Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

If there is an integer not less than 0 satisfying the following conditions, print the smallest such integer; otherwise, print `-1`.

* The integer has exactly N digits in base ten. (We assume 0 to be a 1-digit integer. For other integers, leading zeros are not allowed.)
* The s_i-th digit from the left is c_i. \left(i = 1, 2, \cdots, M\right)

Constraints

* All values in input are integers.
* 1 \leq N \leq 3
* 0 \leq M \leq 5
* 1 \leq s_i \leq N
* 0 \leq c_i \leq 9

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N M
s_1 c_1
\vdots
s_M c_M


Output

Print the answer.

Examples

Input

3 3
1 7
3 2
1 7


Output

702


Input

3 2
2 1
2 3


Output

-1


Input

3 1
1 0


Output

-1

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest N-digit integer that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The integer has exactly N digits (0 is considered 1-digit)
3. We're given M constraints: the s_i-th digit from the left should be c_i
4. If no such integer exists, return -1

## Key Observations

1. For a valid N-digit number:
   - If N > 1, the first digit cannot be 0
   - If N = 1, the digit can be 0

2. The constraints must be consistent:
   - If we have conflicting constraints (same position with different values), it's impossible
   - If we have a constraint that forces a position to be 0 when it's the first digit and N > 1, it's impossible

3. To get the smallest possible number:
   - We should fill all positions with 0 by default
   - Then apply the given constraints
   - But we need to ensure the first digit is not 0 (when N > 1)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read N and M
2. Store the constraints in a dictionary or list
3. Create an array of N digits, initially all 0
4. Apply each constraint to the appropriate position
5. Check for consistency:
   - If any position has conflicting constraints (same position with different values)
   - If the first digit (position 1) is 0 and N > 1
6. If consistent, return the number; otherwise return -1

## Edge Cases

- N = 1: first digit can be 0
- Multiple constraints on the same position: must be equal
- First digit is 0 when N > 1: invalid

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** N=3, M=3, constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- Position 1: 7 (from both constraints)
- Position 3: 2
- Position 2: 0
- Number: 702 ✓

**Example 2:** N=3, M=2, constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- Both constrain position 2: 1 and 3 → conflict → -1 ✓

**Example 3:** N=3, M=1, constraint: (1,0)
- Position 1: 0, but N=3 > 1 → first digit cannot be 0 → -1 ✓

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Store constraints
constraints = {}
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit()
    constraints[s] = c

# Create digit array (1-indexed, positions 1 to N)
digits = [0] * (N + 1)  # index 0 unused, positions 1 to N

# Apply constraints
for pos, digit in constraints.items():
    digits[pos] = digit

# Check if first digit is 0 when N > 1
if N > 1 and digits[1] == 0:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Convert to number
    result = 0
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        result = result * 10 + digits[i]
    print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. The problem says "if there is an integer not less than 0 satisfying the conditions". Let me reconsider.

Actually, my current logic has a flaw. I'm checking for conflicts when I read the constraints, but I need to check all constraints first, then verify the first digit constraint.

Let me revise:

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Store constraints
constraints = {}
conflicting = False

for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            conflicting = True
            break
    constraints[s] = c

# If there's a conflict, return -1
if conflicting:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create digit array (1-indexed, positions 1 to N)
    digits = [0] * (N + 1)  # index 0 unused, positions 1 to N
    
    # Apply constraints
    for pos, digit in constraints.items():
        digits[pos] = digit
    
    # Check if first digit is 0 when N > 1 (first digit can't be 0 for N > 1)
    if N > 1 and digits[1] == 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Convert to number
        result = 0
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            result = result * 10 + digits[i]
        print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest integer with exactly N digits that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The constraints specify that at position s_i (from left, 1-indexed), the digit must be c_i
3. If such an integer exists, return the smallest one; otherwise return -1
4. Leading zeros are not allowed (so first digit can't be 0)
5. For N=1, 0 is considered a 1-digit integer

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** N=3, M=3, constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- We need a 3-digit number
- Position 1 (first digit) must be 7
- Position 3 (last digit) must be 2
- The third constraint repeats the first, so it's consistent
- So the number is 7_2, and we need to find the smallest such number
- The middle digit (position 2) has no constraints, so we can set it to 0
- Result: 702

**Example 2:** N=3, M=2, constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- Position 2 must be both 1 and 3 - contradiction
- No such number exists → return -1

**Example 3:** N=3, M=1, constraint: (1,0)
- Position 1 must be 0, but this would make it a 1-digit number (0), not a 3-digit number
- 0 is considered 1-digit, so we can't have a 3-digit number starting with 0
- No such number exists → return -1

## Algorithm

1. Read N and M
2. Store all constraints in a dictionary (position -> digit)
3. Check for contradictions:
   - If same position has different digit constraints → contradiction
4. Check if first digit (position 1) is 0 → this would make it invalid for N > 1
5. For positions without constraints, we can set them to 0 to get the smallest number
6. Build the number by placing digits at their positions
7. Return the number or -1 if impossible

Actually, let me reconsider the contradiction check:
- If two constraints have the same position but different digits → contradiction
- If the first digit is constrained to 0 and N > 1 → contradiction (since we can't have leading zeros)
- For positions without constraints, we set them to 0 (to get smallest number)

But what about other positions? For N-digit numbers, only the first digit can't be 0. All other positions can have 0.

Let me formalize:

1. Create a dictionary `constraints` mapping position → digit
2. Iterate through constraints to check for duplicates at same position
3. If any position has multiple conflicting digits → return -1
4. If position 1 has digit 0 and N > 1 → return -1 (leading zero not allowed)
5. For positions without constraints, set them to 0
6. Build the N-digit number by placing the constraints and 0 for the rest
7. Return the number

So algorithm:
- Read inputs
- Store constraints in a dict: position -> digit
- Check for contradictions in constraints (same position, different digits)
- Check if N > 1 and position 1 has digit 0 → return -1
- For all positions from 1 to N:
  - If position has a constraint, use that digit
  - Otherwise, use 0
- But what if the position is 1 and N=1 and digit is 0? This should be valid
- Only constraint is that for N>1, first digit can't be 0

- No position conflicts
- N=3>1, pos1=7≠0 → valid

- Number: 702 ✓

Example 2: N=3, constraints: pos2=1, pos2=3
- Same position (2) has different digits (1 and 3) → contradiction → -1 ✓

Example 3: N=3, constraint: pos1=0
- N=3>1, pos1=0 → leading zero → invalid → -1 ✓

One more edge case: what if no constraints? Then we need the smallest N-digit number.
- For N=1: 0
- For N>1: the smallest N-digit number is 10...0 with N digits → first digit 1, rest 0

Wait, but if N=1 and no constraints, what should it be?
- According to problem: 0 is a 1-digit integer, so 0 should be valid
- For N>1, smallest number with N digits starts with 1 (not 0)

So for no constraints:
- If N = 1: return 0
- If N > 1: return 10^(N-1) (which is 1 followed by N-1 zeros)

But is that correct? The problem says "smallest such integer", so yes.

But wait, do we have any constraints that might force the first digit to be something else? If not, we can set it to the minimal valid value.

Actually, in our algorithm above, if there are no constraints, we need to handle position 1 specially.

So let me refine:

For each position i from 1 to N:
- If position i has a constraint, use that digit
- Otherwise, if i == 1 and N > 1, set to 1 (minimal for first digit of N-digit number)
- Otherwise (i > 1), set to 0

But what if i == 1 and N == 1? Then set to 0.

So general rule:
- For position 1:
  - If it has a constraint, use that digit
  - Else if N == 1, set to 0
  - Else (N > 1), set to 1
- For positions 2 to N:
  - If has constraint, use that digit
  - Else, set to 0

But what about contradictions in constraints? We already check that.

And we check that same position doesn't have conflicting digits.

Actually, in the constraints input, if we have two lines with same s_i but different c_i, that's a contradiction.

So algorithm:

1. Read N, M
2. Create dict `pos_to_digit` to store constraints
3. For each of the M lines:
   - Read s_i, c_i
   - If s_i already exists in dict, check if c_i matches the existing digit. If not, return -1
   - Otherwise, add (s_i, c_i)
4. If N > 1 and position 1 has digit 0, return -1
5. Build the number:
   - Create array of N digits (index 0 to N-1, representing positions 1 to N)
   - For position from 1 to N:
      - If position has a constraint, set digit at (position-1) to that digit
      - Else:
          - If position == 1 and N > 1, set to 1
          - Else (position > 1), set to 0
6. Convert array to integer and return

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Dictionary to store position -> digit constraints
constraints = {}

# Process M constraints
contradiction = False
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            contradiction = True
            break
    constraints[s] = c

# If contradiction found, output -1
if contradiction:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Build the number as a list of digits
    digits = [0] * N  # digits[i] represents position i+1
    
    # Fill in the constraints
    for pos, digit in constraints.items():
        digits[pos - 1] = digit
    
    # Fill in the rest with minimal digits (0 except first digit when N>1)
    if N > 1:
        # For first digit (position 1), if not already set, set to 1
        if digits[0] == 0:
            digits[0] = 1
    else:
        # N == 1, we'll handle below
        pass

    # But in our constraint processing, if no constraint and N==1, digits[0] is still 0
    # That's correct for N==1

    # But wait - what if first digit is constrained to 0 when N>1?
    # We need to check that before setting to 1
    
    # Actually, in the constraint processing, if first digit is constrained to 0 and N>1, we don't catch it until later
    # So we need to check: if N>1 and constraints have pos1=0, that's invalid
    
    # Let me fix: after processing constraints, check if any first position constraint exists and is 0
    if N > 1:
        if 1 in constraints and constraints[1] == 0:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # If first digit is not constrained or constrained to non-zero, and N>1
            # Check if first digit is 0 in our digits array
            if digits[0] == 0:
                digits[0] = 1
    
    # Also need to check that if first digit is not constrained and N>1, we set it to 1
    # But in our code above, we only set it if digits[0] == 0 and N>1
    # But what if there's no constraint and N>1? We need to set first digit to 1
    
    # Actually, if first digit is not in constraints, and N>1, we need to set it to 1
    # Our current code only does this if digits[0] == 0
    # But digits[0] might be 0 because it wasn't set to anything
    
    # Actually, in our initialization, we set all to 0
    # So if first digit has no constraint, digits[0] = 0
    # So when N>1, we check if digits[0] == 0, then set to 1

    # We already have that check: if N>1 and 1 in constraints and constraints[1]==0 → invalid
    
    # So after these checks, we build the number
    # But wait, what if N==1 and first digit is constrained to 0? That's fine
    
    # Actually, in the constraint processing, if N==1 and first digit is constrained to 0, that's valid
    
    # So the complete check should be:
    # If N > 1 and (1 in constraints and constraints[1] == 0) → contradiction
    # And if N > 1 and first digit is not constrained, we set it to 1
    
    # But we already check for contradiction if first digit is 0 in constraints when N>1
    
    # Let's restructure the check after constraint processing:
    
    # Actually, I need to do this check only once:
    if N > 1:
```

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Dictionary to store position -> digit constraints
constraints = {}

# Process M constraints
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit(0)
    constraints[s] = c

# Check if there's a constraint on position 1 with digit 0 when N > 1
if N > 1 and 1 in constraints and constraints[1] == 0:
    print(-1)
    exit(0)

# Build the number as a list of digits
digits = [0] * N  # digits[i] represents position i+1

# Fill in the constraints
for pos, digit in constraints.items():
    digits[pos - 1] = digit

# If N > 1, and the first digit is still 0 (no constraint), set it to 1
if N > 1:
    if digits[0] == 0:
        digits[0] = 1

# Build the result number
result = 0
for digit in digits:
    result = result * 10 + digit

print(result)
```

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Dictionary to store position -> digit constraints
constraints = {}

# Process M constraints
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit(0)
    constraints[s] = c

# Check if there's a constraint on position 1 with digit 0 when N > 1
if N > 1 and 1 in constraints and constraints[1] == 0:
```

for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:

            exit(0)
    constraints[s] = c

if N > 1 and 1 in constraints and constraints[1] == 0:
    print(-1)
    exit(0)

digits = [0] * N  # digits[i] represents position i+1

for pos, digit in constraints.items():

# If N > 1, and the first digit is still 0 (no constraint), set it to 1
if N > 1:

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest integer with exactly N digits that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The constraints specify that the s_i-th digit from the left should be c_i
3. If no such integer exists, return -1
4. Leading zeros are not allowed (so first digit can't be 0)
5. For N=1, 0 is considered a 1-digit integer

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:**
- N=3, M=3
- Constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- This means: 1st digit = 7, 3rd digit = 2, 1st digit = 7
- So the number is 7_2 where the middle digit is not constrained
- To minimize, we want the smallest possible middle digit, which is 0
- Result: 702

**Example 2:**
- N=3, M=2
- Constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- This means: 2nd digit should be both 1 and 3 → impossible
- Result: -1

**Example 3:**
- N=3, M=1
- Constraint: (1,0)
- This means: 1st digit = 0
- But first digit cannot be 0 (no leading zeros)
- Result: -1

## Algorithm

1. Read N and M
2. Store all constraints in a dictionary where key is position (1-indexed) and value is the required digit
3. Check for conflicts: if the same position has different required digits, return -1
4. Check if the first digit (position 1) is 0 → if yes, return -1 (leading zero not allowed)
5. Build the number:
   - Initialize a list of N digits (all 0 initially)
   - For each constraint, set the digit at position s_i to c_i
   - For positions with no constraints, set to 0 (to minimize)
6. Convert to integer and return

Wait, there's a subtle point: if we have conflicting constraints, we need to catch that.

## Implementation Steps

1. Read input
2. Create a dictionary to store constraints
3. Check for conflicting constraints (same position with different values)
4. Validate first digit is not 0
5. Build the result number
6. Return result or -1

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Dictionary to store position -> digit constraints
constraints = {}

# Read M constraints
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit()
    constraints[s] = c

# Check if first digit is 0 (leading zero not allowed)
if 1 in constraints and constraints[1] == 0:
    print(-1)
    exit()

# Build the number
# Create a list of N digits (positions 1 to N, 1-indexed)
result_digits = [0] * N  # index 0 = 1st digit, index 1 = 2nd digit, etc.

# Set the constrained digits
for pos, digit in constraints.items():
    # pos is 1-indexed, so convert to 0-indexed
    result_digits[pos - 1] = digit

# The rest of the digits (unconstrained) are set to 0 (to minimize)
# Since we're building the smallest number, we only need to set the ones we know

# Convert to integer and print
result = 0
for i in range(N):
    result = result * 10 + result_digits[i]

print(result)
```

- Second constraint: pos 2, digit 3 → constraints[2] already exists and is 1, so 1 != 3 → conflict → print -1 ✓

Example 3: N=3, M=1, constraint (1,0)
- constraints[1] = 0
- First digit is 0 → leading zero → print -1 ✓

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest integer with exactly N digits that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The integer must have exactly N digits (0 is considered 1-digit)
3. We have M constraints: the s_i-th digit from the left must be c_i
4. If such an integer exists, return the smallest one; otherwise return -1

## Key Observations

1. **Contradictions**: If we have two constraints for the same position, they must have the same digit value
2. **Leading zeros**: The first digit (leftmost) cannot be 0 for N > 1 (since we need exactly N digits)
3. **Smallest possible number**: To get the smallest number, we should minimize digits from left to right
4. **Position constraints**: The s_i-th digit must be c_i

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values
2. Check for duplicate constraints at the same position - if they differ, return -1
3. Build the digit string with all positions initialized to 0
4. Apply the given constraints
5. Check if the first digit is 0 (for N > 1) - if so, return -1
6. If all constraints are satisfied and the number has exactly N digits, return it; otherwise return -1

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: N=3, M=3, constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- Position 1: must be 7
- Position 3: must be 2
- Position 1: again 7 (consistent)
- So number is 702 (positions: 1:7, 2:0, 3:2)
- This is valid, so answer is 702

**Example 2**: N=3, M=2, constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- Both constraints say position 2 must be 1 and 3 - contradiction
- Return -1

**Example 3**: N=3, M=1, constraint: (1,0)
- Position 1 must be 0, but this is a 3-digit number, so first digit cannot be 0
- Return -1

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Dictionary to store digit constraints
constraints = {}

# Read all constraints
for _ in range(m):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    if s in constraints:
        if constraints[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit()
    else:
        constraints[s] = c

# Build the result string
result_digits = ['0'] * n

# Apply constraints
for pos, digit in constraints.items():
    # pos is 1-indexed, convert to 0-indexed
    result_digits[pos - 1] = str(digit)

# Check if first digit is 0 for n > 1
if n > 1 and result_digits[0] == '0':
    print(-1)
else:
    # Convert to integer and print
    result_num = int(''.join(result_digits))
    print(result_num)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest integer with exactly N digits that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The integer must have exactly N digits (0 is considered 1-digit)
3. We're given M constraints where the s_i-th digit from the left must be c_i
4. If such an integer exists, print the smallest one; otherwise print -1

## Key observations

1. **Leading zeros are not allowed** - so the first digit cannot be 0 (except for the special case of 0 itself when N=1)
2. **Multiple constraints on the same position** - if we have conflicting constraints (like position 1 must be both 7 and 7, or 7 and 8), it's impossible
3. **We need the smallest possible integer** - this means we should try to make leftmost digits as small as possible, but still satisfy constraints
4. **For positions without constraints** - we can set them to 0 (since we want the smallest number)

## Step-by-step solution

1. **Input parsing**: Read N, M, then M pairs of (s_i, c_i)
2. **Handle constraints**:
   - Create a list to store the digits of the N-digit number
   - For each constraint (s_i, c_i), position s_i-1 (0-indexed) should be c_i
   - If we have conflicting constraints for the same position, return -1
3. **Check for valid first digit**:
   - If N > 1, the first digit (position 0) cannot be 0
   - If N == 1, then the digit can be 0
4. **Build the number**:
   - Fill in the constrained digits
   - Fill in other positions with 0 (to make it smallest)
5. **Check validity**:
   - If any position has conflicting constraints → return -1
   - If first digit is 0 and N > 1 → return -1
   - Otherwise, return the constructed number

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: N=3, M=3, constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- Position 1 (0-indexed): must be 7 (from two constraints)

**Example 3**: N=3, M=1, constraint: (1,0)
- Position 1 (0-indexed): must be 0
- First digit is 0, but N=3 > 1 → invalid → output -1

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest N-digit integer that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The integer must have exactly N digits (0 is considered a 1-digit number)
3. We have M constraints: the s_i-th digit from the left must be c_i
4. If no such integer exists, return -1

## Key Observations

1. **Position conflicts**: If two constraints specify the same position but different digits, it's impossible
2. **Leading zeros**: The first digit (leftmost) cannot be 0 (except for the case where N=1 and the digit is 0)
3. **Digit assignment**: For positions that have constraints, we must assign the specified digit
4. **Unassigned positions**: We need to fill them with the smallest possible digits (0 for non-leading positions)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Parse input**: Read N, M, and the constraints
2. **Check for position conflicts**: If same position has different digits, return -1
3. **Check leading zero constraint**: If position 1 has digit 0 and N > 1, return -1 (since first digit can't be 0 for multi-digit numbers)
4. **Build the result**: Create an array of N digits, initialize with 0s
5. **Apply constraints**: Set the specified positions to their required digits
6. **Check for conflicts**: After applying constraints, verify that no position has conflicting values
7. **Fill remaining positions**: For positions without constraints, set them to 0 (which gives the smallest number)
8. **Validate**: Ensure the resulting number has exactly N digits and satisfies all constraints

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: N=3, M=3, constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- Position 1 gets 7, position 3 gets 2
- Position 2 has no constraint, so it gets 0
- Result: 702 ✓

**Example 2**: N=3, M=2, constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- Both constraints say position 2, but different digits (1 vs 3)
- Conflict → return -1 ✓

**Example 3**: N=3, M=1, constraint: (1,0)
- Position 1 is 0, but N=3 (3-digit number)
- First digit can't be 0 → return -1 ✓

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())
constraints = []
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    constraints.append((s, c))

# Create a dictionary to store digit constraints by position
position_digit = {}
for s, c in constraints:
    if s in position_digit:
        # Conflict at this position
        if position_digit[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit()
    position_digit[s] = c

# Check if first digit (position 1) is 0 and N > 1
if N > 1 and position_digit.get(1, 0) == 0:
    print(-1)
    exit()

# Build the result as an array of N digits (1-indexed positions)
result = [0] * (N + 1)  # 1-indexed for easier access

# Apply constraints
for s, c in constraints:
    result[s] = c

# Fill remaining positions with 0 (smallest possible)
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    if result[i] == 0:
        # This position has no constraint, so we put 0
        pass
    # We already have constraints applied above

# Convert to number
# Since we have constraints and filled with 0s, the result should be valid
# But we need to verify no conflicts and leading zero issue
# We already checked conflicts and leading zero above

# Construct the number as a string
digits = []
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    if i == 1 and N > 1 and result[i] == 0:
        # This should have been caught above
        print(-1)
        exit()
    digits.append(str(result[i]))

# Let's recheck: if there's a constraint conflict, we already returned -1

    if s in position_digit:
        if position_digit[s] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit()
    result[s] = c

# Check if position 1 is 0 and N > 1
if N > 1 and result[1] == 0:
    print(-1)
    exit()

# Build the final number string
number_str = ""
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    number_str += str(result[i])

print(number_str)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

If there is an integer not less than 0 satisfying the following conditions, print the smallest such integer; otherwise, print `-1`.

* The integer has exactly N digits in base ten. (We assume 0 to be a 1-digit integer. For other integers, leading zeros are not allowed.)
* The s_i-th digit from the left is c_i. \left(i = 1, 2, \cdots, M\right)

Constraints

* All values in input are integers.
* 1 \leq N \leq 3
* 0 \leq M \leq 5
* 1 \leq s_i \leq N
* 0 \leq c_i \leq 9

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N M
s_1 c_1
\vdots
s_M c_M


Output

Print the answer.

Examples

Input

3 3
1 7
3 2
1 7


Output

702


Input

3 2
2 1
2 3


Output

-1


Input

3 1
1 0


Output

-1

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest integer with exactly N digits that satisfies given digit constraints
2. The constraints specify that at position `s_i` (from left, 1-indexed), the digit must be `c_i`
3. If no such integer exists, return -1
4. Leading zeros are not allowed (so first digit can't be 0)
5. Special case: 0 is considered a 1-digit integer

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** N=3, M=3, constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- We need a 3-digit number where:
  - 1st digit = 7
  - 3rd digit = 2
  - 1st digit = 7 (redundant)
- So the number must be 7 _ 2
- To minimize, we set middle digit to 0 → 702
- This satisfies all constraints and is the smallest possible

**Example 2:** N=3, M=2, constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- We need a 3-digit number where:
  - 2nd digit = 1
  - 2nd digit = 3
- Contradiction! Can't have 2nd digit equal to both 1 and 3
- So answer is -1

**Example 3:** N=3, M=1, constraint: (1,0)
- We need a 3-digit number where 1st digit = 0
- But 3-digit numbers can't start with 0 (leading zeros not allowed)
- So answer is -1

## Solution Strategy

1. Read N and M
2. Store all constraints in a dictionary or list
3. Check for conflicting constraints (same position has different digits)
4. Check if the first digit constraint is 0 (if N > 1, first digit can't be 0)
5. Build the number by:
   - Creating a list of N digits, all initialized to 0
   - For each constraint, set the digit at position s_i-1 (0-indexed) to c_i
   - If any position has conflicting constraints, return -1
   - If first digit is 0 and N > 1, return -1
   - Otherwise, form the number and return it

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

# Store constraints
constraints = []
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())
    constraints.append((s, c))

# Check for conflicting constraints
digit_positions = {}
for s, c in constraints:
    pos = s - 1  # convert to 0-indexed
    if pos in digit_positions:
        if digit_positions[pos] != c:
            print(-1)
            exit()
    else:
        digit_positions[pos] = c

# Check if first digit is 0 (for N > 1)
if N > 1 and digit_positions.get(0, 0) == 0:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Build the number
    result_digits = [0] * N
    
    # Set the known digits from constraints
    for s, c in constraints:
        pos = s - 1
        result_digits[pos] = c
    
    # If no constraints for first digit and N > 1, we need to set first digit
    # But actually, if no constraints, we can set first digit to minimum (1) and others to 0
    # However, we need the smallest valid number
    
    # For the remaining positions (except first), set to 0 if not specified
    # But we must ensure that if N > 1 and first digit is 0, it's invalid
    # We already checked that first digit isn't 0 when N > 1
    
    # If first digit isn't set by constraints, we need to set it
    if 0 not in digit_positions:
        # First digit must be at least 1 for N > 1
        if N == 1:
            result_digits[0] = 0
        else:
            result_digits[0] = 1
    else:
        # First digit is already set
        pass
    
    # For remaining positions, if not set, set to 0 (to minimize)
    for i in range(1, N):
        if i not in digit_positions:
            result_digits[i] = 0
    
    # Convert to number
    result = 0
    for digit in result_digits:
        result = result * 10 + digit
    
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the smallest integer with exactly N digits that satisfies certain digit constraints
2. The constraints specify that at position `s_i` (from left, 1-indexed), the digit must be `c_i`
3. If such an integer exists, return the smallest one; otherwise return -1
4. Leading zeros are not allowed (so the first digit can't be 0 for N > 1)
5. 0 is considered a 1-digit integer

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:**
- N=3, M=3
- Constraints: (1,7), (3,2), (1,7)
- This means: 1st digit = 7, 3rd digit = 2, 1st digit = 7
- So the number is 7 _ 2
- We need to fill the middle digit (2nd position)
- Since we want the smallest number, we set middle digit to 0
- Result: 702

**Example 2:**
- N=3, M=2
- Constraints: (2,1), (2,3)
- This means: 2nd digit = 1 AND 2nd digit = 3
- Contradiction! 1 ≠ 3
- So no such number exists → output -1

**Example 3:**
- N=3, M=1
- Constraint: (1,0)
- This means: 1st digit = 0
- But for a 3-digit number, first digit can't be 0 (would make it a 2-digit number)
- So no such number exists → output -1

## Algorithm

1. Read N and M
2. Store all constraints in a dictionary or list
3. Check for conflicts: if two constraints say the same position has different digits, return -1
4. Check if the first digit constraint exists and is 0 (for N > 1) → if so, return -1
5. If no conflicts and first digit isn't 0 (or if N=1 and first digit is 0), construct the number
6. For positions without constraints, set them to 0 (to get the smallest number)
7. If N=1 and first digit is 0, 0 is allowed (since 0 is considered 1-digit)

## Implementation Steps

1. Read input
2. Store constraints in a dictionary: position → digit
3. Check for conflicts in constraints
4. Check first digit constraint for N > 1
5. Create the result array of N digits
6. Fill in constraints
7. Fill remaining positions with 0
8. Convert to integer and return, or -1 if invalid

```python
    exit()

# Step 3: Create the result array of N digits, initially all 0
result = [0] * N

# Step 4: Fill in the constraints
for pos, digit in constraints.items():
    result[pos] = digit

# Step 5: For positions without constraints, we set to 0 (to get smallest number)
# But this is already done - we initialized to 0

# Step 6: Check if the first digit is 0 and N > 1
# We already checked above, so it's safe

# Step 7: Convert to integer and print
answer = 0
for i in range(N):
    answer = answer * 10 + result[i]

print(answer)
```

Wait, there's an issue with the input reading. I'm reading the constraints twice. Let me fix that.

```python
# Read input
N, M = map(int, input().split())

constraints = {}

# Read M constraints
for _ in range(M):
    s, c = map(int, input().split())

    if pos in constraints:
        if constraints[pos] != c:
            print(-1)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string with given costs
3. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical
4. If it's impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. For positions where `s[i] != t[i]`, we need to change at least one of the characters
2. We can change a character to any other character using the given transformation rules
3. This becomes a shortest path problem - we need to find the cheapest way to transform characters from one to another
4. We need to find the minimum cost to transform both strings to a common target string

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Build a transformation graph
- Create a graph where nodes are characters (a-z)
- Add edges based on the given transformations (Ai → Bi with cost Wi)
- We need to find shortest paths between all pairs of characters

### Step 2: Find shortest paths between all character pairs
- Use Floyd-Warshall or Dijkstra's algorithm since we have at most 26 characters
- Floyd-Warshall is suitable here as we have only 26 nodes

### Step 3: For each position, calculate the minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
- For each position i, we need to transform either s[i] or t[i] (or both) to the same character
- For each possible target character c, calculate cost to transform s[i] to c + cost to transform t[i] to c
- The minimum cost for position i is the minimum over all possible c

### Step 4: Check if transformation is possible
- We need to ensure that for each character, we can eventually reach any other character (connectivity)
- If any character is isolated (can't reach any other character), return -1

### Step 5: Output the result
- Sum up minimum costs for all positions
- Output the resulting string by choosing the optimal target character for each position

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
import heapq

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Create transformation rules
    rules = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        rules.append((a, b, w))
    
    # If strings have different lengths, it's impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Create adjacency matrix for character transformations
    # dist[i][j] = minimum cost to transform char i to char j
    # Initialize with infinity
    chars = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
    char_to_idx = {char: i for i, char in enumerate(chars)}
    n_chars = 26
    
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [[INF] * n_chars for _ in range(n_chars)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal (cost to transform a char to itself is 0)
    for i in range(n_chars):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Add given transformations
    for a, b, w in rules:
        ai = char_to_idx[a]
        bi = char_to_idx[b]
        dist[ai][bi] = min(dist[ai][bi], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(n_chars):
        for i in range(n_chars):
            for j in range(n_chars):
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Check if all characters are connected (at least one path between any two)
    # For each character, check if it can reach any other character
    reachable = [False] * n_chars
    for i in range(n_chars):
        for j in range(n_chars):
            if dist[i][j] != INF:
                reachable[i] = True
                break
    
    # If any character is not reachable from others, it's impossible
    # Actually, we need to check if for each position, both s[i] and t[i] can be transformed to some character
    # So we need to check if both characters can reach a common character
    # For each position i, we need to check if there exists a character c such that both s[i] and t[i] can reach c
    total_cost = 0
    result_str = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        si = char_to_idx[s[i]]
        ti = char_to_idx[t[i]]
        
        # Check if there exists a character c that both si and ti can reach
        min_cost_for_position = INF
        best_char = None
        
        # Try each possible target character
        for c in range(n_chars):
            # Cost to transform s[i] to c
            cost_s_to_c = dist[si][c]
            # Cost to transform t[i] to c
            cost_t_to_c = dist[ti][c]
            
            # Total cost if both are transformed to c
            total_cost_to_c = cost_s_to_c + cost_t_to_c
            
            # If we can reach c from both s[i] and t[i]
            if cost_s_to_c != INF and cost_t_to_c != INF:
                if total_cost_to_c < min_cost_for_position:
                    min_cost_for_position = total_cost_to_c
                    best_char = c
        
        # If no character can be reached from both, it's impossible
        if min_cost_for_position == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost_for_position
        result_str.append(chars[best_char])
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result_str))

solve()
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the connectivity check. The issue is that even if individual characters can reach some characters, we might not be able to transform both strings to a common character.

Actually, the Floyd-Warshall will handle this correctly - if dist[si][c] and dist[ti][c] are both finite, then we can transform both to c.

- Position 1: a vs x - need to transform a to x or x to a
- From rules: a->x cost 8, x->y cost 13, d->c cost 3
- To get a to x: cost 8
- To get x to a: no direct rule, but x->y->? - we don't have y->x or any path from x to a
- So can we transform both? a to x (cost 8) + x to x (cost 0) = 8, or x to y (13) + a to y? 
- Actually, we need to see if a can reach x, x can reach a, etc.

In the example, we have:
- a -> x (cost 8)
- x -> y (cost 13)
- d -> c (cost 3)

- Option 1: transform a to x (cost 8), t[i]=x stays (cost 0) → total cost 8
- Option 2: transform x to x (cost 0), a to x (cost 8) → same
- But can we transform x to a? No direct rule, and no path shown in the rules
- How about a to y? a->x->y cost 8+13=21, then x to y: cost 0 for x to y? No, we're transforming x to y, so if we transform x to y, and a to y, cost = 21
- Actually, we're transforming individual characters, so for position 1, if we change s[1]=a to y, and t[1]=x to y, cost = (a->y cost) + (x->y cost)

But do we have a->y? Only through a->x->y with cost 21, and x->y with cost 13, so a->y = 8+13=21, x->y=13.

So cost for a->y + x->y = 21 + 13 = 34, which is worse than a->x (8) + x->x (0) = 8.

So minimum is 8.

But the expected output is 21 for the total cost.

Wait, position 3: s[3]=d, t[3]=d, so already same.

But s[1] is a, t[1] is x. To make both x, we need to change a to x (cost 8) or x to a (cost ?). We don't have x->a. 

How do we get cost 21? 

Looking again: example says output is 21 and uxyd.

So positions:
- Position 0: u vs u → cost 0
- Position 1: a vs x → if we change a to x: cost 8
- Position 2: y vs x → already different, need to change
- Position 3: d vs d → cost 0

Position 2: s[2]=y, t[2]=x
We need to make them equal. Can we transform y to x or x to y?

We have x->y cost 13, but no y->x. So if we transform x to y, cost 13, and y to y, cost 0 → total cost 13
Or transform y to something else, x to something else.

So we need to transform both to some character c.

Available characters: 
- If to a: a->y cost 8+13=21, x->a: from x to y to a? No path to a.
- If to b: no rules
- If to c: no rules
- If to d: no rules
- If to e: no rules

Only a can go to x, x can go to y.

So for position 2: s[2]=y, t[2]=x
- To transform y to x: no path
- To transform x to y: cost 13, y to y: cost 0 → total 13
- To transform y to z: no info
- The only possibility is x to y, y to y → cost 13

So for position 2: cost 13
Position 1: a to x → cost 8
Total cost: 8+13 = 21. That matches!

So my algorithm should work. I just need to make sure the Floyd-Warshall correctly computes the shortest path through transformation chains.

The algorithm is correct. I'll finalize it.

```python
import sys

def main():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # If strings have different lengths, it's impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Create transformation rules
    rules = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        rules.append((a, b, w))
    
    # Character set
    chars = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
    char_to_idx = {char: i for i, char in enumerate(chars)}
    n_chars = 26
    
    # Distance matrix for character transformations
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [[INF] * n_chars for _ in range(n_chars)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal (cost to transform a char to itself is 0)
    for i in range(n_chars):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Add given transformations
    for a, b, w in rules:
        ai = char_to_idx[a]
        bi = char_to_idx[b]
        dist[ai][bi] = min(dist[ai][bi], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall algorithm to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(n_chars):
        for i in range(n_chars):
            for j in range(n_chars):
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Verify all characters are reachable from each other (though not all need to be, only that for each position pair there's a common target)
    total_cost = 0
    result_chars = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        si = char_to_idx[s[i]]
        ti = char_to_idx[t[i]]
        
        min_cost = INF
        best_char_idx = -1
        
        # Try every possible character as the target
        for target in range(n_chars):
            # Cost to transform s[i] to target
            cost_s = dist[si][target]
            # Cost to transform t[i] to target
            cost_t = dist[ti][target]
            
            # If both transformations are possible (finite cost)
            if cost_s != INF and cost_t != INF:
                total_cost_for_target = cost_s + cost_t
                if total_cost_for_target < min_cost:
                    min_cost = total_cost_for_target
                    best_char_idx = target
        
        # If no valid target found for this position
        if min_cost == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        result_chars.append(chars[best_char_idx])
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result_chars))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

            cost_s = dist[si][target]
            # Cost to transform t[i] to target

    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result_chars))

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string using given transformation rules
3. Each transformation rule `(Ai, Bi, Wi)` means we can change character `Ai` to `Bi` at cost `Wi`
4. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical, and output the resulting string
5. If it's impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. We need to find the minimum cost to transform both strings to some common target string
2. For each position `i`, we need to find the minimum cost to make `s[i]` and `t[i]` equal
3. This is a shortest path problem where each character is a node and transformation rules create edges with weights
4. We need to find the minimum cost to transform any character in the first string to match the corresponding character in the second string

## Solution Approach

1. Build a graph where nodes are characters (a-z)
2. For each transformation rule `(Ai, Bi, Wi)`, add an edge from `Ai` to `Bi` with weight `Wi`
3. Use Floyd-Warshall or Dijkstra to find shortest distances between all pairs of characters
4. For each position, calculate the minimum cost to make `s[i]` and `t[i]` equal by finding the shortest path between them
5. Sum up all these costs and find the optimal target string

Wait, actually there's a simpler approach. Since we want both strings to be identical, at each position i, we need to make `s[i]` and `t[i]` the same. The optimal choice is the character that minimizes the total transformation cost.

For each position i, we need to find the minimum cost to convert both `s[i]` and `t[i]` to some common character `c`. This means we need to find the character `c` that minimizes `dist(s[i], c) + dist(t[i], c)`.

However, since we can only transform characters according to the given rules, we need to find the shortest path between any two characters in the transformation graph.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Parse input and build transformation rules
2. Build a graph of characters with edge weights
3. Use Floyd-Warshall algorithm to find shortest distances between all pairs of characters
4. For each position, try all possible target characters and find the one that minimizes total cost
5. Sum up the costs and determine the resulting string
6. If for any position the minimum cost is infinite (no path exists), return -1

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
import heapq

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build graph - distances between characters
    # Initialize with infinity, except same character has distance 0
    chars = set()
    chars.add(s[0])
    chars.add(t[0])
    
    # Add all characters from both strings
    for c in s:
        chars.add(c)
    for c in t:
        chars.add(c)
    
    # Add all transformation rules
    rules = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        rules.append((a, b, w))
        chars.add(a)
        chars.add(b)
    
    # Convert chars to list
    char_list = list(chars)
    char_to_idx = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(char_list)}
    
    # Initialize distance matrix
    size = len(char_list)
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [[INF] * size for _ in range(size)]
    
    # Diagonal is 0
    for i in range(size):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Add direct transformation rules
    for a, b, w in rules:
        ia = char_to_idx[a]
        ib = char_to_idx[b]
        dist[ia][ib] = min(dist[ia][ib], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(size):
        for i in range(size):
            for j in range(size):
                if dist[i][k] != INF and dist[k][j] != INF:
                    dist[i][j] = min(dist[i][j], dist[i][k] + dist[k][j])
    
    # Check if the transformation graph is connected for all needed characters
    # For each position, find the minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if len(s) != len(t):
            print(-1)
            return
        
        c1 = s[i]
        c2 = t[i]
        
        # Find the best character to transform both to
        min_cost = INF
        best_char_idx = -1
        
        for j in range(size):
            char = char_list[j]
            cost1 = dist[char_to_idx[c1]][j]
            cost2 = dist[char_to_idx[c2]][j]
            
            if cost1 == INF or cost2 == INF:
                continue
                
            total_cost_here = cost1 + cost2
            if total_cost_here < min_cost:
                min_cost = total_cost_here
                best_char_idx = j
        
        if min_cost == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        result.append(char_list[best_char_idx])
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

```python
import sys

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # If strings have different lengths, it's impossible to make them identical
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build graph - distances between characters
    chars = set()
    for c in s:
        chars.add(c)
    for c in t:
        chars.add(c)
    
    # Add all transformation rules
    rules = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        rules.append((a, b, w))
        chars.add(a)
        chars.add(b)
    
    # Convert chars to list
    char_list = list(chars)
    char_to_idx = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(char_list)}
    
    # Initialize distance matrix
    size = len(char_list)
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [[INF] * size for _ in range(size)]
    
    # Diagonal is 0

        dist[i][i] = 0

    for k in range(size):
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Boy Valera likes strings. And even more he likes them, when they are identical. That's why in his spare time Valera plays the following game. He takes any two strings, consisting of lower case Latin letters, and tries to make them identical. According to the game rules, with each move Valera can change one arbitrary character Ai in one of the strings into arbitrary character Bi, but he has to pay for every move a particular sum of money, equal to Wi. He is allowed to make as many moves as he needs. Since Valera is a very economical boy and never wastes his money, he asked you, an experienced programmer, to help him answer the question: what minimum amount of money should Valera have to get identical strings. 

Input

The first input line contains two initial non-empty strings s and t, consisting of lower case Latin letters. The length of each string doesn't exceed 105. The following line contains integer n (0 ≤ n ≤ 500) — amount of possible changings. Then follow n lines, each containing characters Ai and Bi (lower case Latin letters) and integer Wi (0 ≤ Wi ≤ 100), saying that it's allowed to change character Ai into character Bi in any of the strings and spend sum of money Wi.

Output

If the answer exists, output the answer to the problem, and the resulting string. Otherwise output -1 in the only line. If the answer is not unique, output any.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string with given costs
3. Each move costs `Wi` to change character `Ai` to `Bi`
4. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical
5. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. For each position `i`, we have `s[i]` and `t[i]` - these should become the same character
2. We need to find the cheapest way to convert both characters to some common character
3. This is essentially a shortest path problem where we have character transformation costs
4. We can use Floyd-Warshall or Dijkstra to find the minimum cost to transform any character to any other character
5. For each position, we find the minimum cost to make `s[i]` and `t[i]` the same

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Build a transformation cost matrix**: Create a 26×26 matrix where `cost[i][j]` represents the minimum cost to change character `i` to character `j`
2. **Initialize the matrix**: Set diagonal to 0 (same character costs 0), and set `cost[a][b] = w` for each given transformation
3. **Fill in all possible transformations**: Use Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
4. **For each position**: Find minimum cost to make `s[i]` and `t[i]` the same character by trying all 26 possibilities
5. **Check feasibility**: If for any position, no transformation exists (infinite cost), return -1
6. **Output**: Total minimum cost and the resulting string

- Option 1: both become 'x': cost = cost(a→x) + cost(x→x) = 8 + 0 = 8
- Option 2: both become 'y': cost = cost(a→y) + cost(x→y) = (a→x→y = 8+13) + 13 = 34
- Option 3: both become 'd': cost = ???

Input: uayd, uxxd
Output: 21, uxyd

So:
- uayd → uxyd: a→x (cost 8)
- uxxd → uxyd: x at position 2 (index 2) → y (cost 13)
Wait: positions: 
- 0: u→u
- 1: a→x (cost 8)
- 2: y→y? no, t[2] is x, should become y (cost 13)
- 3: d→d

Wait the result is uxyd, so t was uxxd → uxyd: index 2: x→y (cost 13)

So total cost = cost to change s[1] from a to x (8) + cost to change t[2] from x to y (13) = 21

So for each position i, we can independently decide:
- Either change s[i] to some character c
- Or change t[i] to some character c  
- Or change both to some character c

But we want to minimize total cost.

Actually, we can transform either or both strings at each position.

The key insight is that for each position i, we can:
- Keep s[i] and t[i] as they are (if already equal)
- Change s[i] to c and leave t[i] unchanged
- Change t[i] to c and leave s[i] unchanged  
- Change both to c

The cost for position i when both become c is: cost(s[i]→c) + cost(t[i]→c)

But we might also only change one of them, which is equivalent to making them both become the same character c.

Actually, if we change only s[i] to c, then t[i] stays as is, so they are not equal unless t[i] = c. If we change only t[i] to c, then s[i] stays as is, so they are equal only if s[i] = c.

So to make them equal, we need either:
1. Change s[i] to c and t[i] to c (both to c)
2. Change s[i] to t[i] (cost 0) 
3. Change t[i] to s[i] (cost 0)

Wait, no - if we change s[i] to t[i], and t[i] stays, then both become t[i] - this costs cost(s[i]→t[i])
If we change t[i] to s[i], and s[i] stays, then both become s[i] - this costs cost(t[i]→s[i])

So for each position, the minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal is:
- min over all characters c of (cost(s[i]→c) + cost(t[i]→c)) 
- OR (cost(s[i]→t[i]) if we only change s[i]) + 0 (if t[i] stays)
- OR (cost(t[i]→s[i]) if we only change t[i]) + 0 (if s[i] stays)

But if we change s[i] to t[i], the cost is cost(s[i]→t[i]) and we get both characters equal to t[i]
If we change t[i] to s[i], the cost is cost(t[i]→s[i]) and we get both equal to s[i]
If we change both to some c, cost is cost(s[i]→c) + cost(t[i]→c)

So the minimum cost for position i is:
min( cost(s[i]→t[i]), cost(t[i]→s[i]), min over c of (cost(s[i]→c) + cost(t[i]→c)) )

But notice that when c = t[i], cost(s[i]→t[i]) + cost(t[i]→t[i]) = cost(s[i]→t[i]) + 0 = cost(s[i]→t[i])
When c = s[i], cost(s[i]→s[i]) + cost(t[i]→s[i]) = 0 + cost(t[i]→s[i]) = cost(t[i]→s[i])

So the min over c of (cost(s[i]→c) + cost(t[i]→c)) will include the cost(s[i]→t[i]) and cost(t[i]→s[i]) cases.

Actually, it will always include them as special cases when c = t[i] and c = s[i].

Therefore, for each position i, the minimum cost is:
min_c (cost(s[i]→c) + cost(t[i]→c))

Because when c = t[i], we get cost(s[i]→t[i]) + cost(t[i]→t[i]) = cost(s[i]→t[i]) + 0
When c = s[i], we get cost(s[i]→s[i]) + cost(t[i]→s[i]) = 0 + cost(t[i]→s[i])

So the minimum over all c will be at most the minimum of cost(s[i]→t[i]) and cost(t[i]→s[i]), and potentially less if there's a better c.

Therefore, algorithm:
1. Build a 26x26 cost matrix for character transformations
2. Use Floyd-Warshall to find shortest distances between all character pairs
3. For each position i, compute cost_i = min over c in 0-25 of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c])
4. If any cost_i is infinity, return -1
5. Total cost = sum of all cost_i
6. For each position, find the character c that achieves the minimum cost in step 3, then update both strings to c

But wait - in the transformation, when we make both strings have character c at position i, we change s[i] to c and t[i] to c.

So we need to track what character each string becomes at each position.

## Implementation Steps

1. Read input
2. Initialize 26x26 matrix with infinity, diagonal 0
3. For each transformation, set cost[Ai][Bi] = min(cost[Ai][Bi], Wi)
4. Apply Floyd-Warshall to find all-pairs shortest paths
5. For each position, find min cost to make both characters equal
6. Sum all costs and construct resulting string

Note: We only consider direct and indirect transformations via Floyd-Warshall.

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Initialize cost matrix for 26 letters
    # cost[i][j] = minimum cost to change char i to char j
    cost = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    for i in range(26):
        cost[i][i] = 0
    
    # Read transformations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        a = line[0]
        b = line[1]
        w = int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('b')
        # Update cost to minimum
        cost[a_idx][b_idx] = min(cost[a_idx][b_idx], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all characters
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if cost[i][k] + cost[k][j] < cost[i][j]:
                    cost[i][j] = cost[i][k] + cost[k][j]
    
    # Check if any transformation is still infinite
    # For each position, find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
    total_cost = 0
    result_s = []
    result_t = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if len(t) == 0 or i >= len(t):
            print(-1)
            return
        if len(s) == 0 or i >= len(s):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        s_char = s[i]
        t_char = t[i]
        s_idx = ord(s_char) - ord('a')
        t_idx = ord(t_char) - ord('b')
        
        # Find minimum cost to make both equal to some character c
        min_cost_at_pos = float('inf')
        best_char = -1
        
        for c in range(26):
            c_char = chr(c + ord('a'))
            # cost to change s[i] to c + cost to change t[i] to c
            current_cost = cost[s_idx][c] + cost[t_idx][c]
            if current_cost < min_cost_at_pos:
                min_cost_at_pos = current_cost
                best_char = c
        
        # If minimum cost is still inf, no valid transformation exists
        if min_cost_at_pos == float('inf'):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost_at_pos
        result_s.append(chr(best_char + ord('a')))
        result_t.append(chr(best_char + ord('a')))
    
    # Check if both strings have the same length after processing
    if len(result_s) != len(result_t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result_s))

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # If lengths are different, impossible to make identical
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Initialize cost matrix for 26 letters
    cost = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    for i in range(26):
        cost[i][i] = 0
    
    # Read transformations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        a = line[0]
        b = line[1]
        w = int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('b')
        cost[a_idx][b_idx] = min(cost[a_idx][b_idx], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all characters
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if cost[i][k] + cost[k][j] < cost[i][j]:
                    cost[i][j] = cost[i][k] + cost[k][j]
    
    # For each position, find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        s_idx = ord(s[i]) - ord('a')
        t_idx = ord(t[i]) - ord('a')
        
        min_cost_at_pos = float('inf')
        best_char = -1
        
        for c in range(26):
            current_cost = cost[s_idx][c] + cost[t_idx][c]
            if current_cost < min_cost_at_pos:
                min_cost_at_pos = current_cost
                best_char = c
        
        if min_cost_at_pos == float('inf'):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost_at_pos
        result.append(chr(best_char + ord('a')))
```

Let me test with the first example:
- s = "uayd", t = "uxxd"
- Transformations: a→x(8), x→y(13), d→c(3)

After Floyd-Warshall:
- cost[a][x] = 8 initially, others inf
- cost[x][y] = 13 initially
- cost[d][c] = 3 initially

After Floyd-Warshall:
- cost[a][y] = cost[a][x] + cost[x][y] = 8+13 = 21
- cost[x][c] = inf (no path)
- cost[d][c] = 3
- cost[a][c] = inf
- etc.

Position 0: u vs u
- For each c: cost[u][c] + cost[u][c] = 2 * cost[u][c]
- cost[u][u] = 0, so cost = 0
- min cost = 0, best_char = u

Position 1: a vs x
- For c: cost[a][c] + cost[x][c]
- c=u: cost[a][u] + cost[x][u] = inf + inf = inf
- c=x: cost[a][x] + cost[x][x] = 8 + 0 = 8
- c=y: cost[a][y] + cost[x][y] = 21 + 13 = 34  
- c=c: cost[a][c] + cost[x][c] = inf + inf = inf
- So min cost = 8, best_char = x

Position 2: y vs x  
- For c: cost[y][c] + cost[x][c]
- c=u: inf + inf = inf
- c=x: cost[y][x] + cost[x][x] = inf + 0 = inf
- c=y: cost[y][y] + cost[x][y] = 0 + 13 = 13
- c=c: cost[y][c] + cost[x][c] = inf + inf = inf
- So min cost = 13, best_char = y

Position 3: d vs d
- Cost: 2 * cost[d][c]
- c=d: 2*0 = 0, so cost 0
- So min cost 0, best_char = d

Total cost = 0 + 8 + 13 + 0 = 21 ✓

Resulting string: u + x + y + d = "uxyd" ✓

Perfect!

Second example:
- s = "a", t = "b"
- Transformations: a→b(2), a→b(3), b→a(5)

After Floyd-Warshall:
- cost[a][b] = 2
- cost[b][a] = 5

For position 0: a vs b
- c=a: cost[a][a] + cost[b][a] = 0 + 5 = 5
- c=b: cost[a][b] + cost[b][b] = 2 + 0 = 2
- So min cost = 2, best_char = b
- Total cost = 2
- Result: "b" ✓

Third example:
- s = "abc", t = "ab"
- Lengths are different! 3 vs 2 → impossible to make identical

Wait, input says:
Input:
abc
ab
6
a b 4
a b 7
b a 8
c b 11
c a 3
a c 0

So s="abc", t="ab" → different lengths → output should be -1

In the expected output, it says -1 ✓

So my code should work.

Final code:

```python
s = input().strip()
t = input().strip()
n = int(input().strip())

# If lengths are different, impossible
if len(s) != len(t):
    print(-1)
else:
    # Initialize cost matrix for 26 letters
    cost = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    for i in range(26):
        cost[i][i] = 0
    
    # Read transformations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        a = line[0]
        b = line[1]
        w = int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('b')
        cost[a_idx][b_idx] = min(cost[a_idx][b_idx], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all characters
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if cost[i][k] + cost[k][j] < cost[i][j]:
                    cost[i][j] = cost[i][k] + cost[k][j]
    
    # For each position, find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        s_idx = ord(s[i]) - ord('a')
        t_idx = ord(t[i]) - ord('a')
        
        min_cost_at_pos = float('inf')
        best_char = -1
        
        for c in range(26):
            current_cost = cost[s_idx][c] + cost[t_idx][c]
            if current_cost < min_cost_at_pos:
                min_cost_at_pos = current_cost
                best_char = c
        
        if min_cost_at_pos == float('inf'):
            print(-1)
            exit()
            
        total_cost += min_cost_at_pos
        result.append(chr(best_char + ord('a')))
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string with given costs
3. Each operation changes one character Ai to Bi with cost Wi
4. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical, and output the resulting string
5. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. We need to make both strings identical, so we need to find a target string that both can be converted to with minimum total cost
2. For each position i, if s[i] ≠ t[i], we need to change at least one of them to match
3. We can think of this as: for each position, we can change s[i] to some character c, or t[i] to some character c
4. The target character at position i should be chosen to minimize the total cost
5. This becomes a shortest path problem where we need to find the minimum cost to convert each character to any other character

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read input strings and operations
2. Build a graph of character transformations with their costs
3. For each position, find the minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
4. Use Floyd-Warshall or Dijkstra to find shortest paths between all character pairs
5. For each position, if we need to change characters, find the optimal target character that minimizes cost
6. If for any position both original characters can't be converted to the same character, return -1
7. Otherwise, calculate total cost and output the result

## Implementation Details

Since we have at most 26 lowercase letters, we can use Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs.

1. Initialize a distance matrix with infinity, and 0 for same characters
2. For each operation, update the cost to transform Ai to Bi
3. Run Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
4. For each position i:
   - If s[i] == t[i], cost = 0, target = s[i]
   - If s[i] ≠ t[i], try all possible target characters c, and calculate cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
   - Choose the target character that minimizes this cost
5. Sum all position costs to get total minimum cost

Let me trace through the first example:
- s = "uayd", t = "uxxd"
- Operations: a→x (8), x→y (13), d→c (3)

For position 0: u vs u → cost 0, target u
For position 1: a vs x → need to make both equal
  - Make both to a: cost = dist[a][a] + dist[x][a]. We don't have x→a in any direct operation, and no path from x to a is given. So we need to consider all possible paths.
  - Actually, we have a→x (8), but no x→a. So x→a would require x→y→? and we don't have y→a. So x→a is impossible?
  - Wait, let me reconsider. From the operations, we have a→x (8), x→y (13), but no reverse. So we can go a→x, but not x→a.
  - To make a and x equal, we need to make both go to some common character.
  - Option 1: both to a → cost = 0 (a→a) + (x→a) = 0 + infinity = infinity
  - Option 2: both to x → cost = (a→x)=8 + (x→x)=0 = 8  
  - Option 3: both to y → cost = (a→y) + (x→y). a→y: a→x→y = 8+13=21, x→y=13, so total 21+13=34
  - Option 4: both to c → cost = (a→c) + (x→c). We don't have these.
  - So minimum is 8 when both become x.

Wait, this seems wrong. The expected output is 21 for "uxyd", which suggests target at position 1 is y, position 2 is x→x? Let me check.

Actually looking at the expected output: "uxyd" 
- Position 0: u→u (same)
- Position 1: a→x? No, a→x would make it x, but output is x
- Position 1: original a vs x → if we change a to x: cost 8, then both are x
- Position 2: y vs x → y vs x, need to change one to match. If we change y to x? But we don't have y→x operation. We have x→y, not y→x.
- The output is "uxyd", so position 2: y→y? But original t has x at position 2.

I think I made a mistake. Original strings are "uayd" and "uxxd". Output is "uxyd":
- Position 0: u vs u → same
- Position 1: a vs x → becomes x, so a→x (cost 8)
- Position 2: y vs x → becomes y? No, output has y at position 2, so original t has x at position 2, and we need to convert x to y (cost 13)
- Position 3: d vs d → same

Total cost = 8 + 13 = 21. Yes! So we can choose a→x (cost 8) and x→y (cost 13).

So the algorithm should find, for each position, the minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal by possibly changing either s[i] or t[i] (or both) to some common character c.

Therefore, for each position i, we compute:
cost[i] = min over all characters c of (dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c])

We build the distance matrix first using Floyd-Warshall.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
import heapq

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Initialize distance matrix (26 characters, 'a' to 'z')
    # dist[i][j] = minimum cost to change char i to char j
    dist = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal to 0
    for i in range(26):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process operations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        dist[a_idx][b_idx] = min(dist[a_idx][b_idx], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Check if any transformation is impossible (infinite cost)
    # For each position, find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if len(s) != len(t):
            print(-1)
            return
        
        s_char = s[i]
        t_char = t[i]
        
        s_idx = ord(s_char) - ord('a')
        t_idx = ord(t_char) - ord('a')
        
        # If already the same
        if s_idx == t_idx:
            total_cost += 0
            result.append(s_char)
        else:
            # Find minimum cost to make both characters equal to some common character c
            min_cost = float('inf')
            best_char = None
            
            for c in range(26):
                cost = dist[s_idx][c] + dist[t_idx][c]
                if cost < min_cost:
                    min_cost = cost
                    best_char = c
            
            # If impossible to make them equal
            if min_cost == float('inf'):
                print(-1)
                return
            
            total_cost += min_cost
            result.append(chr(ord('a') + best_char))
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # If lengths are different, impossible to make identical
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Initialize distance matrix (26 characters, 'a' to 'z')
    # dist[i][j] = minimum cost to change char i to char j
    dist = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal to 0
    for i in range(26):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process operations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        dist[a_idx][b_idx] = min(dist[a_idx][b_idx], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Check if any transformation is impossible (infinite cost)
    # For each position, find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        s_char = s[i]
        t_char = t[i]
        
        s_idx = ord(s_char) - ord('a')
        t_idx = ord(t_char) - ord('a')
        
        # If already the same
        if s_idx == t_idx:
            total_cost += 0
            result.append(s_char)
        else:

            min_cost = float('inf')
            best_char = None
            
            for c in range(26):
                cost = dist[s_idx][c] + dist[t_idx][c]

                    min_cost = cost
                    best_char = c
```
