Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string with given costs
3. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical
4. If it's impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. For positions where `s[i] != t[i]`, we need to make them equal
2. We can change either character in either string
3. The cost depends on the transformation rules provided
4. We need to find the cheapest way to make all corresponding positions equal

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the transformation rules
- We have n transformation rules: change Ai to Bi with cost Wi
- We need to find the shortest path (minimum cost) to transform any character to any other character
- This is essentially a shortest path problem on a graph where nodes are characters (a-z)

### Step 2: Build a graph and find shortest paths
- Create a graph where each lowercase letter is a node
- Add directed edges from Ai to Bi with cost Wi
- Use Floyd-Warshall or Dijkstra to find shortest distances between all pairs of characters

### Step 3: For each position, calculate minimum cost
- For each position i where s[i] != t[i], we need to transform either s[i] or t[i] to match
- We can transform s[i] to t[i], or transform t[i] to s[i], or transform both to some intermediate character
- We need to find the minimum cost among all possible ways

### Step 4: Handle impossible cases
- If there's no path from any character to any other character, return -1

### Step 5: Implementation approach
1. Use Floyd-Warshall to compute shortest path between all pairs of characters
2. For each position where characters differ, calculate minimum cost to make them equal
3. Sum all minimum costs
4. Construct the final string

Let me implement this:

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
import heapq

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # If strings have different lengths, impossible to make identical
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Build transformation graph
    # dist[a][b] = minimum cost to transform char a to char b
    # Initialize with infinity
    chars = set('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz')
    dist = {}
    for a in chars:
        dist[a] = {}
        for b in chars:
            dist[a][b] = float('inf')
    
    # Set diagonal to 0 (same character takes 0 cost)
    for c in chars:
        dist[c][c] = 0
    
    # Add given transformation rules
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        dist[a][b] = min(dist[a][b], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    # Since we have only 26 characters, Floyd-Warshall is efficient
    char_list = list(chars)
    for k in char_list:
        for i in char_list:
            for j in char_list:
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Check if any transformation is impossible
    # If for any character, there's no path to some other character, we need to check
    # But we'll just compute the minimum cost for each position
    
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i] == t[i]:
            result.append(s[i])
            continue
            
        # Find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
        # We can transform s[i] to t[i], or t[i] to s[i], or both to some common character
        min_cost = float('inf')
        
        # Option 1: transform s[i] to t[i]
        cost1 = dist[s[i]][t[i]]
        
        # Option 2: transform t[i] to s[i]
        cost2 = dist[t[i]][s[i]]
        
        # Option 3: transform both to some intermediate character c
        # Transform s[i] to c (cost dist[s[i]][c]) and t[i] to c (cost dist[t[i]][c])
        # Total cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
        # We want to minimize this over all c
        
        min_common = float('inf')
        for c in char_list:
            cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
            if cost < min_common:
                min_common = cost
                
        # The minimum cost is the minimum of direct transformations and common transformations
        min_cost = min(cost1, cost2, min_common)
        
        # If min_cost is still infinity, it means we can't transform these characters to match
        if min_cost == float('inf'):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        
        # Choose the final character (we can choose any valid one)
        # For simplicity, if cost1 is minimum, we make s[i] -> t[i]
        # If cost2 is minimum, we make t[i] -> s[i]
        # If min_common is minimum, we make both to some c
        
        # Check which option gives minimum cost
        if cost1 <= cost2 and cost1 <= min_common:
            result.append(t[i])
        elif cost2 <= cost1 and cost2 <= min_common:
            result.append(s[i])
        else:
            # Find a character c that minimizes dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
            min_common_cost = float('inf')
            best_c = None
            for c in char_list:
                cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
                if cost < min_common_cost:
                    min_common_cost = cost
                    best_c = c
            result.append(best_c)
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # If strings have different lengths, impossible to make identical
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # Build transformation graph
    # dist[a][b] = minimum cost to transform char a to char b
    # Initialize with infinity
    chars = set('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz')
    dist = {}
    for a in chars:
        dist[a] = {}
        for b in chars:
            dist[a][b] = float('inf')
    
    # Set diagonal to 0 (same character takes 0 cost)
    for c in chars:
        dist[c][c] = 0
    
    # Add given transformation rules
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        dist[a][b] = min(dist[a][b], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    # Since we have only 26 characters, Floyd-Warshall is efficient
    char_list = sorted(list(chars))
    for k in char_list:
        for i in char_list:
            for j in char_list:
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Check if any transformation is impossible
    # We'll just proceed, and if a cost is inf, we'll catch it
    
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i] == t[i]:
            result.append(s[i])
            continue
            
        # Find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
        min_cost = float('inf')
        
        # Option 1: transform s[i] to t[i]
        cost1 = dist[s[i]][t[i]]
        
        # Option 2: transform t[i] to s[i]
        cost2 = dist[t[i]][s[i]]
        
        # Option 3: transform both to some common character c
        # Total cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
        min_common = float('inf')
        for c in chars:
            cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
            if cost < min_common:
                min_common = cost
                
        # The minimum cost is the minimum of these options
        min_cost = min(cost1, cost2, min_common)
        
        # If min_cost is still infinity, it means we can't transform these characters to match
        if min_cost == float('inf'):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        
        # Choose the final character (we can choose any valid one)
        # We'll just pick a character that achieves the minimum cost
        best_char = None
        
        # Check if cost1 is minimum
        if cost1 == min_cost:
            best_char = t[i]
        # Check if cost2 is minimum
        elif cost2 == min_cost:
            best_char = s[i]
        # Check if min_common is minimum
        else:
            # Find a character c that minimizes dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
            min_common_cost = float('inf')
            for c in chars:
                cost = dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c]
                if cost == min_common:
                    best_char = c
                    break
        
        if best_char is None:
            # Fallback - just use one of the original
            best_char = s[i]
            
        result.append(best_char)
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

```python
        elif cost2 == min_cost:
            best_char = s[i]

                    break
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Boy Valera likes strings. And even more he likes them, when they are identical. That's why in his spare time Valera plays the following game. He takes any two strings, consisting of lower case Latin letters, and tries to make them identical. According to the game rules, with each move Valera can change one arbitrary character Ai in one of the strings into arbitrary character Bi, but he has to pay for every move a particular sum of money, equal to Wi. He is allowed to make as many moves as he needs. Since Valera is a very economical boy and never wastes his money, he asked you, an experienced programmer, to help him answer the question: what minimum amount of money should Valera have to get identical strings. 

Input

The first input line contains two initial non-empty strings s and t, consisting of lower case Latin letters. The length of each string doesn't exceed 105. The following line contains integer n (0 ≤ n ≤ 500) — amount of possible changings. Then follow n lines, each containing characters Ai and Bi (lower case Latin letters) and integer Wi (0 ≤ Wi ≤ 100), saying that it's allowed to change character Ai into character Bi in any of the strings and spend sum of money Wi.

Output

If the answer exists, output the answer to the problem, and the resulting string. Otherwise output -1 in the only line. If the answer is not unique, output any.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string with given costs
3. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical
4. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. For each position `i`, if `s[i] != t[i]`, we need to make them equal
2. We can change either `s[i]` to `t[i]` or `t[i]` to `s[i]` or change both to some intermediate character
3. We need to find the minimum cost to make all positions equal
4. This becomes a shortest path problem where characters are nodes and edge weights are transformation costs

## Approach

1. Build a graph where nodes are lowercase letters (a-z)
2. For each transformation rule (A→B with cost W), add an edge from A to B with weight W
3. Use Floyd-Warshall or Dijkstra to find shortest path between any two characters
4. For each position where `s[i] != t[i]`, find the minimum cost to transform both characters to the same character
5. Sum up all minimum costs across positions

Wait, actually we don't need to transform both to the same character - we just need them to become equal at each position. For each position, we can:
- Keep them as they are (if already equal)
- Change s[i] to t[i] (if possible)
- Change t[i] to s[i] (if possible) 
- Change both to some third character c (if possible)

But actually, for each position, we need to make s[i] and t[i] equal. The minimum cost to make them equal is the minimum cost among:
- Transform s[i] to t[i] directly (if available)
- Transform t[i] to s[i] directly (if available)
- Transform both to some common character c (where cost = dist(s[i], c) + dist(t[i], c))

However, since we can only apply the given operations, we need to find the shortest path between any two characters.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build a matrix of shortest distances between all pairs of letters (using Floyd-Warshall)
2. For each position i where s[i] != t[i]:
   - Find the minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
   - This is min over all characters c of (dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c])
3. Sum all these minimum costs
4. Also need to determine the resulting string - we can choose any valid final character

Important: If there's no path between two characters, it's impossible.

Wait, the problem states that we have operations to change A→B with cost W. But we don't have all possible transformations. So we need to build the transformation graph.

Actually, if we can't transform from one character to another, then it's impossible to make the strings identical at that position.

But for a specific position, if there's no path from s[i] to some character c and from t[i] to c, then it's impossible.

Actually, for each position i where s[i] != t[i], we need to find if there exists a character c such that we can transform s[i] to c and t[i] to c with some total cost.

So:
1. Build graph of characters with given transformations
2. Find shortest distances between all pairs of characters using Floyd-Warshall (only for reachable characters)
3. For each position i where s[i] != t[i]:
   - If no character c exists such that both dist[s[i]][c] and dist[t[i]][c] are finite, return -1
   - Otherwise, find min over c of (dist[s[i]][c] + dist[t[i]][c])
4. Sum all these minimum costs
5. Construct the resulting string by choosing for each position the character that minimizes the cost (or any that achieves the minimum)

## Implementation

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict
import heapq

def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build graph for character transformations
    # dist[i][j] = minimum cost to transform char i to char j
    # Use 0-indexed: 'a' -> 0, 'b' -> 1, etc.
    chars = [chr(ord('a') + i) for i in range(26)]
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [[INF] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal
    for i in range(26):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process transformations
    for _ in range(n):
        parts = input().strip().split()
        a = parts[0]
        b = parts[1]
        w = int(parts[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        dist[a_idx][b_idx] = w
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if dist[i][k] != INF and dist[k][j] != INF:
                    dist[i][j] = min(dist[i][j], dist[i][k] + dist[k][j])
    
    # Check if the problem is solvable
    # For each position, if s[i] != t[i], we need to find a common character c
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(min(len(s), len(t))):
        if s[i] == t[i]:
            result.append(s[i])
            continue
            
        # Find minimum cost to make s[i] and t[i] equal
        min_cost = INF
        best_char = None
        
        # Try all possible target characters
        for c in chars:
            c_idx = ord(c) - ord('a')
            cost_to_s = dist[ord(s[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx]
            cost_to_t = dist[ord(t[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx]
            
            if cost_to_s != INF and cost_to_t != INF:
                total_cost_for_pair = cost_to_s + cost_to_t
                if total_cost_for_pair < min_cost:
                    min_cost = total_cost_for_pair
                    best_char = c
        
        if min_cost == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        result.append(best_char)
    
    # If one string is longer than the other, we need to handle the suffix
    # For positions beyond the shorter string
    if len(s) > len(t):
        # We can only change characters in string s, and we need to make s[i] = t[i] for all i < len(t)
        # For the remaining characters in s, we need to change them to match the current result or to some value
        # But our goal is to make both strings identical, so we need to change the extra characters in s
        # Actually, we can only change characters in the strings, and we can change any character to any other with given costs
        # So for each position i >= len(t), we have s[i] to match with t[i] (which doesn't exist)
        # This means we need to change s[i] to some character that we might use for the rest of the string
        # But since we want identical strings, we need to have the same length
        
        # The problem doesn't specify what to do with unequal lengths
        # Looking at the examples: "abc" and "ab" - output is -1
        
        # In example 3: "abc" and "ab" -> output -1
        # So if the strings have different lengths, it's impossible to make them identical?
        
        # Yes! Because we can only change characters, not remove or add them
        # So the resulting strings must have the same length as both original strings
        
        print(-1)
        return
        
    elif len(t) > len(s):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # But the first example has same length, second has same length
    # Actually in input: "a" and "b" - both length 1
    # In third example: "abc" and "ab" - different lengths
    
    # So if lengths differ, impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    # We have the result for each position
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))
```

But there's a bug in the Floyd-Warshall: I only updated if dist[i][k] and dist[k][j] are finite, but I didn't initialize all to INF. And I should run Floyd-Warshall properly.

Actually, with the given rules, if there's no path, dist[i][j] stays INF.

Let me fix the code:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build graph for character transformations
    # dist[i][j] = minimum cost to transform char i to char j
    chars = [chr(ord('a') + i) for i in range(26)]
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [[INF] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal
    for i in range(26):
        dist[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process transformations
    for _ in range(n):
        parts = input().strip().split()
        a = parts[0]
        b = parts[1]
        w = int(parts[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        dist[a_idx][b_idx] = w
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if dist[i][k] != INF and dist[k][j] != INF:
                    if dist[i][j] > dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]:
                        dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
    
    # Check if the problem is solvable
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    # Check length first
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i] == t[i]:
            result.append(s[i])
            continue
            
        min_cost = INF
        best_char = None
        
        # Try all possible target characters
        for c in chars:
            c_idx = ord(c) - ord('a')
            cost_to_s = dist[ord(s[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx]
            cost_to_t = dist[ord(t[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx]
            
            if cost_to_s != INF and cost_to_t != INF:
                total_cost_for_pair = cost_to_s + cost_to_t
                if total_cost_for_pair < min_cost:
                    min_cost = total_cost_for_pair
                    best_char = c
        
        if min_cost == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        result.append(best_char)
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Boy Valera likes strings. And even more he likes them, when they are identical. That's why in his spare time Valera plays the following game. He takes any two strings, consisting of lower case Latin letters, and tries to make them identical. According to the game rules, with each move Valera can change one arbitrary character Ai in one of the strings into arbitrary character Bi, but he has to pay for every move a particular sum of money, equal to Wi. He is allowed to make as many moves as he needs. Since Valera is a very economical boy and never wastes his money, he asked you, an experienced programmer, to help him answer the question: what minimum amount of money should Valera have to get identical strings. 

Input

The first input line contains two initial non-empty strings s and t, consisting of lower case Latin letters. The length of each string doesn't exceed 105. The following line contains integer n (0 ≤ n ≤ 500) — amount of possible changings. Then follow n lines, each containing characters Ai and Bi (lower case Latin letters) and integer Wi (0 ≤ Wi ≤ 100), saying that it's allowed to change character Ai into character Bi in any of the strings and spend sum of money Wi.

Output

If the answer exists, output the answer to the problem, and the resulting string. Otherwise output -1 in the only line. If the answer is not unique, output any.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string using available operations
3. Each operation allows us to change character A to character B at cost W
4. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical, and output the resulting string
5. If it's impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. We need to find the minimum cost to transform both strings into the same target string
2. For each position i, we have character s[i] in string s and t[i] in string t
3. We need to decide what character to convert both s[i] and t[i] to (let's call it c)
4. The cost for position i will be: cost(s[i] → c) + cost(t[i] → c)
5. We want to minimize the total cost across all positions

## Algorithm Steps

1. Parse input to get strings s, t, and operations
2. Build a cost matrix to store the minimum cost to convert any character to any other character
3. For each position, try all possible target characters (a-z) and calculate the total cost
4. Choose the target character that minimizes the cost for that position
5. Sum up costs across all positions
6. Check if the transformation is possible (if we can actually reach any character from all starting characters)

## Implementation Details

1. First, we need to determine if each character can be transformed to any other character (connected components in the transformation graph)
2. For each position, we find the best target character by trying all 26 lowercase letters
3. We need to handle the case where certain transformations are impossible

Let me implement this:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build operations list
    operations = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        operations.append((a, b, w))
    
    # Build graph of possible transformations
    # graph[a] = list of (b, cost) pairs
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for a, b, w in operations:
        graph[a].append((b, w))
    
    # Find minimum cost to convert from any char to any other char using Floyd-Warshall or BFS
    # Since we have at most 26 characters, we can use BFS from each starting character
    
    # cost[i][j] = minimum cost to convert char i to char j
    # Initialize with infinity
    char_to_char_cost = {}
    chars = set()
    for a, b, w in operations:
        chars.add(a)
        chars.add(b)
    
    # Add all lowercase letters that appear in the strings
    for c in s:
        chars.add(c)
    for c in t:
        chars.add(c)
    
    # Add all lowercase letters
    for c in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
        chars.add(c)
    
    chars = list(chars)
    char_map = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(chars)}
    num_chars = len(chars)
    
    # Initialize cost matrix with infinity
    INF = float('inf')
    cost_matrix = [[INF] * num_chars for _ in range(num_chars)]
    
    # Set diagonal to 0
    for i in range(num_chars):
        cost_matrix[i][i] = 0
    
    # Fill in direct operations
    for a, b, w in operations:
        i = char_map[a]
        j = char_map[b]
        cost_matrix[i][j] = min(cost_matrix[i][j], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(num_chars):
        for i in range(num_chars):
            for j in range(num_chars):
                if cost_matrix[i][k] + cost_matrix[k][j] < cost_matrix[i][j]:
                    cost_matrix[i][j] = cost_matrix[i][k] + cost_matrix[k][j]
    
    # Check if cost_matrix is valid - if any entry is still INF, then transformation is impossible
    # But we need to check if we can actually make the strings identical
    
    # For each position, we want to find the target character that minimizes total cost
    total_cost = 0
    result_s = list(s)
    result_t = list(t)
    
    possible = True
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if len(s) != len(t):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        # For position i, we have chars s[i] and t[i]
        s_char = s[i]
        t_char = t[i]
        
        s_idx = char_map[s_char]
        t_idx = char_map[t_char]
        
        # Try each possible target character
        min_cost_for_pos = INF
        best_target_idx = -1
        
        for target_char in chars:
            target_idx = char_map[target_char]
            
            # Cost to convert s[i] to target
            cost_s_to_target = cost_matrix[s_idx][target_idx]
            
            # Cost to convert t[i] to target
            cost_t_to_target = cost_matrix[t_idx][target_idx]
            
            total_cost_for_target = cost_s_to_target + cost_t_to_target
            
            if total_cost_for_target < min_cost_for_pos:
                min_cost_for_pos = total_cost_for_target
                best_target_idx = target_idx
                best_target_char = target_char
        
        # If no valid target found, impossible
        if min_cost_for_pos == INF:
            possible = False
            break
            
        total_cost += min_cost_for_pos
        
        # Update the result strings
        result_s[i] = best_target_char
        result_t[i] = best_target_char
    
    if not possible:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(total_cost)
        print(''.join(result_s))

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. There's a potential issue with the character mapping and the fact that we might not have all 26 letters. Also, the Floyd-Warshall algorithm will work fine since we only have 26 characters.

Let me fix and simplify the solution:

```python
from collections import defaultdict
import sys

def main():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build operations list
    operations = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        operations.append((a, b, w))
    
    # Extract all relevant characters
    chars = set()
    for c in s:
        chars.add(c)
    for c in t:
        chars.add(c)
    for a, b, w in operations:
        chars.add(a)
        chars.add(b)
    
    # Add all lowercase letters
    all_chars = list("abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz")
    chars = list(chars)
    
    # Create character to index mapping
    char_to_idx = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(chars)}
    
    # Initialize cost matrix with infinity
    num_chars = len(chars)
    INF = float('inf')
    cost_matrix = [[INF] * num_chars for _ in range(num_chars)]
    
    # Set diagonal to 0
    for i in range(num_chars):
        cost_matrix[i][i] = 0
    
    # Fill direct operations
    for a, b, w in operations:
        i = char_to_idx[a]
        j = char_to_idx[b]
        cost_matrix[i][j] = min(cost_matrix[i][j], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall algorithm to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(num_chars):
        for i in range(num_chars):
            for j in range(num_chars):
                if cost_matrix[i][k] + cost_matrix[k][j] < cost_matrix[i][j]:
                    cost_matrix[i][j] = cost_matrix[i][k] + cost_matrix[k][j]
    
    # Check if transformation is possible for each position
    total_cost = 0
    result_string = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if len(s) != len(t):
            print(-1)
            return
            
        s_char = s[i]
        t_char = t[i]
        
        s_idx = char_to_idx[s_char]
        t_idx = char_to_idx[t_char]
        
        min_cost = INF
        best_char_idx = -1
        
        # Try all possible target characters
        for target_char in chars:
            target_idx = char_to_idx[target_char]
            cost_to_target_s = cost_matrix[s_idx][target_idx]
            cost_to_target_t = cost_matrix[t_idx][target_idx]
            total_cost_to_target = cost_to_target_s + cost_to_target_t
            
            if total_cost_to_target < min_cost:
                min_cost = total_cost_to_target
                best_char_idx = target_idx
        
        # If no valid target found, impossible
        if min_cost == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        result_string.append(chars[best_char_idx])
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result_string))

main()
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: "uayd" and "uxxd"

Looking at the operations: a→x(8), x→y(13), d→c(3)

- x→y (cost 13), but no y→x

Let me fix the code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def main():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # If lengths are different, impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build operations list
    operations = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a, b, w = line[0], line[1], int(line[2])
        operations.append((a, b, w))
    
    # Extract all relevant characters
    chars = set()
    for c in s:
        chars.add(c)
    for c in t:
        chars.add(c)
    for a, b, w in operations:
        chars.add(a)
        chars.add(b)
    
    # Add all lowercase letters
    all_chars = list("abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz")
    chars = list(chars)
    
    # Create character to index mapping
    char_to_idx = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(chars)}
    
    # Initialize cost matrix with infinity
    num_chars = len(chars)
    INF = float('inf')
    cost_matrix = [[INF] * num_chars for _ in range(num_chars)]
    
    # Set diagonal to 0
    for i in range(num_chars):
        cost_matrix[i][i] = 0
    
    # Fill direct operations
    for a, b, w in operations:
        i = char_to_idx[a]
        j = char_to_idx[b]
        cost_matrix[i][j] = min(cost_matrix[i][j], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall algorithm to find shortest paths between all pairs
    for k in range(num_chars):
        for i in range(num_chars):
            for j in range(num_chars):
                if cost_matrix[i][k] + cost_matrix[k][j] < cost_matrix[i][j]:
                    cost_matrix[i][j] = cost_matrix[i][k] + cost_matrix[k][j]
    
    # Check if transformation is possible for each position
    total_cost = 0
    result_string = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        s_char = s[i]
        t_char = t[i]
        
        s_idx = char_to_idx[s_char]
        t_idx = char_to_idx[t_char]
        
        min_cost = INF
        best_char_idx = -1
        
        # Try all possible target characters
        for target_char in chars:
            target_idx = char_to_idx[target_char]
            cost_to_target_s = cost_matrix[s_idx][target_idx]
            cost_to_target_t = cost_matrix[t_idx][target_idx]
            total_cost_to_target = cost_to_target_s + cost_to_target_t
            
            if total_cost_to_target < min_cost:
                min_cost = total_cost_to_target
                best_char_idx = target_idx
        
        # If no valid target found, impossible
        if min_cost == INF:
            print(-1)
            return
            
        total_cost += min_cost
        result_string.append(chars[best_char_idx])
    
    print(total_cost)
    print(''.join(result_string))

main()
```

This should now handle all cases correctly.

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def main():
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()

    for c in t:
        chars.add(c)

        chars.add(a)
        chars.add(b)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Boy Valera likes strings. And even more he likes them, when they are identical. That's why in his spare time Valera plays the following game. He takes any two strings, consisting of lower case Latin letters, and tries to make them identical. According to the game rules, with each move Valera can change one arbitrary character Ai in one of the strings into arbitrary character Bi, but he has to pay for every move a particular sum of money, equal to Wi. He is allowed to make as many moves as he needs. Since Valera is a very economical boy and never wastes his money, he asked you, an experienced programmer, to help him answer the question: what minimum amount of money should Valera have to get identical strings. 

Input

The first input line contains two initial non-empty strings s and t, consisting of lower case Latin letters. The length of each string doesn't exceed 105. The following line contains integer n (0 ≤ n ≤ 500) — amount of possible changings. Then follow n lines, each containing characters Ai and Bi (lower case Latin letters) and integer Wi (0 ≤ Wi ≤ 100), saying that it's allowed to change character Ai into character Bi in any of the strings and spend sum of money Wi.

Output

If the answer exists, output the answer to the problem, and the resulting string. Otherwise output -1 in the only line. If the answer is not unique, output any.

Examples

Input

uayd
uxxd
3
a x 8
x y 13
d c 3


Output

21
uxyd

a
b
3
a b 2
a b 3
b a 5


Output

b

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings `s` and `t` that we want to make identical
2. We can change characters in either string using given operations
3. Each operation allows changing character `Ai` to `Bi` at cost `Wi`
4. We need to find the minimum cost to make both strings identical, and output the resulting string
5. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. We need to find the minimum cost to transform both strings to some common target string
2. For each position `i`, if `s[i] != t[i]`, we need to change one of them (or both) to make them equal
3. We can model this as a shortest path problem where we want to find the minimum cost to convert each character to another
4. We can use Floyd-Warshall or find minimum transformation costs between all pairs of characters

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Parse input**: Read strings and operations
2. **Build transformation graph**: Create a cost matrix where `cost[a][b]` is the minimum cost to change character `a` to character `b`
3. **Find minimum transformation costs**: Use Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
4. **For each position**: Calculate the cost to make `s[i]` and `t[i]` equal, considering all possible target characters
5. **Find optimal target**: For each position, determine which character to transform both strings to
6. **Check feasibility**: If there's any position where we can't make the characters equal, output -1

## Implementation Details

1. Initialize a cost matrix with infinity (except for direct transformations)
2. For each operation, update the cost matrix
3. Apply Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths
4. For each position, find the minimum cost to equalize s[i] and t[i]
5. Actually, we just need to make the strings identical - we can change both strings to any common character

Wait, let me reconsider: we can change characters in either string. So for position i:
- If s[i] == t[i], cost is 0
- If s[i] != t[i], we need to transform either s[i] to t[i], or transform t[i] to s[i], or transform both to some third character

Actually, since we can transform to any character through a chain of transformations, we need the minimum cost to transform from any character to any other character.

For each position where s[i] != t[i], we can:
- Change s[i] to t[i] with cost `cost[s[i]][t[i]]`
- Change t[i] to s[i] with cost `cost[t[i]][s[i]]`
- Change both to some third character c with cost `cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c]`

We want the minimum of these options across all possible c.

But actually, we can also change one to something else and the other to the same thing. So for each position where s[i] != t[i], we need to find the minimum cost to make them equal.

The minimum cost for position i is: min over all characters c of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c])

But this assumes we transform both characters to c. Is that always optimal?

Actually, it could be that we transform s[i] to t[i] directly (cost cost[s[i]][t[i]]) or transform t[i] to s[i] directly (cost cost[t[i]][s[i]]).

So the minimum cost for position i is: min(cost[s[i]][t[i]], cost[t[i]][s[i]], min over c of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c]))

But if we can transform s[i] to t[i] directly, that might be cheaper than transforming both to some c.

Actually, if we transform both to c, and c equals t[i], then cost is cost[s[i]][t[i]] + cost[t[i]][t[i]] = cost[s[i]][t[i]]. Similarly if c = s[i], cost is cost[s[i]][s[i]] + cost[t[i]][s[i]] = cost[t[i]][s[i]].

So min over c of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c]) will include both the direct transformations.

Therefore, for each position i, the minimum cost is min over all characters c of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c]).

But wait - is that correct? If we transform s[i] to c and t[i] to c, then both become c. This is valid.

We can also transform just one of them, which is equivalent to making c = t[i] or c = s[i].

So yes, min over all c of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c]) gives us the minimum cost for that position.

But we need to ensure that the cost matrix is fully defined. If there's no path from a to b, then cost[a][b] should be infinity.

Also, we can use Floyd-Warshall to compute the shortest path between all pairs.

## Algorithm

1. Read strings s and t
2. Read n operations and build a cost matrix (size 26x26)
3. Initialize cost matrix with a large value (e.g., 10^9)
4. For each operation, set cost[Ai][Bi] = min(cost[Ai][Bi], Wi)
5. Apply Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all pairs
6. For each position i:
   - If s[i] == t[i], cost += 0
   - Else, cost += min over c in 0-25 of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c])
   - Store the resulting character c that achieves this minimum
7. If any position has no valid transformation (cost is infinity), output -1
8. Otherwise output total cost and the resulting string

Wait, actually when we transform both to character c, we get s[i] -> c and t[i] -> c. So the cost is cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c].

But what if we only transform one of them? That's already covered because if c = t[i], cost[s[i]][t[i]] + cost[t[i]][t[i]] = cost[s[i]][t[i]] + 0, which is just cost[s[i]][t[i]].

Similarly if c = s[i], we get cost[s[i]][s[i]] + cost[t[i]][s[i]] = cost[t[i]][s[i]].

So yes, min over all c includes the direct transformations.

However, in our Floyd-Warshall, if there's no path from a to b, cost[a][b] remains large. We need to make sure that for each pair, there's a path.

If for some i, the minimum over c of (cost[s[i]][c] + cost[t[i]][c]) is still infinity, then it's impossible.

Let me trace through the first example:
- s = "uayd", t = "uxxd"
- Operations: a->x cost 8, x->y cost 13, d->c cost 3

Initial cost matrix:
- cost['a']['x'] = 8
- cost['x']['y'] = 13
- cost['d']['c'] = 3

After Floyd-Warshall, we can compute costs between all pairs.

For position 0: s[0]='u', t[0]='u' -> same, cost 0
For position 1: s[1]='a', t[1]='x'
  We need min over c of (cost['a'][c] + cost['x'][c])
  c='a': cost['a']['a'] + cost['x']['a'] = 0 + ? (we don't have this) 
  Actually, cost['x']['a'] should be infinity since no path from x to a
  c='b': cost['a']['b'] + cost['x']['b'] = infinity + infinity
  ... 
  c='x': cost['a']['x'] + cost['x']['x'] = 8 + 0 = 8
  c='y': cost['a']['y'] + cost['x']['y'] = ? + 13. cost['a']['y'] is infinity, so infinity + 13 = infinity
  c='c': cost['a']['c'] + cost['x']['c'] = infinity + infinity
  So minimum is 8 when c='x'

For position 2: s[2]='y', t[2]='x'
  min over c of (cost['y'][c] + cost['x'][c])
  c='y': cost['y']['y'] + cost['x']['y'] = 0 + 13 = 13
  c='x': cost['y']['x'] + cost['x']['x'] = ? + 0. cost['y']['x'] is infinity (no path from y to x), so infinity
  So minimum is 13 when c='y'

For position 3: s[3]='d', t[3]='d' -> same, cost 0

Total cost: 0 + 8 + 13 + 0 = 21
Resulting string: for position 1: both become 'x', so s[1]='x', t[1]='x'
For position 2: both become 'y', so s[2]='y', t[2]='y'

Result: "uxyd" - matches expected output.

Ah! So when strings have different lengths, it's impossible to make them identical because one will be longer than the other.

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # If lengths are different, impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build cost matrix for characters a-z
    # cost[i][j] = minimum cost to change character i to character j
    # Use 0-25 for a-z
    cost = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal to 0
    for i in range(26):
        cost[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process operations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a = line[0]
        b = line[1]
        w = int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        cost[a_idx][b_idx] = min(cost[a_idx][b_idx], w)
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if cost[i][k] + cost[k][j] < cost[i][j]:
                    cost[i][j] = cost[i][k] + cost[k][j]
    
    # Calculate total minimum cost and build result string
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i] == t[i]:
            # Already same, no cost
            result.append(s[i])
        else:
            # Find minimum cost to make both s[i] and t[i] equal to some character c
            min_cost = float('inf')
            best_char = None
            
            # Try each possible character c
            for c_idx in range(26):
                c_char = chr(ord('a') + c_idx)
                current_cost = cost[ord(s[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx] + cost[ord(t[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx]
                if current_cost < min_cost:
                    min_cost = current_cost
                    best_char = c_char
            
            # Add the cost and the resulting character
            total_cost += min_cost
            result.append(best_char)
    
    # Check if total cost is finite (no infinite costs)
    if total_cost == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(total_cost)
        print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

Wait, I realized something: in the Floyd-Warshall, we might have infinite costs. When we compute the minimum for each position, if min_cost is still infinity, then it's impossible.

But in the code above, if for some position, min_cost remains inf, then total_cost will be inf.

So we need to check that min_cost is not inf for any position.

Actually, if there's no path from s[i] to any character or from t[i] to any character, then min_cost will be inf.

So the final check is if total_cost is inf, output -1, else output total_cost and the result.

This should handle all cases.

Let me test with the third example:
s = "abc", t = "ab" - different lengths, so we output -1 immediately. 
But the input says: "abc" and "ab" - different lengths, so should output -1.

Yes, the example output is -1.

So the code should work.

One more thing: in the operations, we have multiple operations with same (a,b) - we take minimum, which is correct.

Also, the Floyd-Warshall handles indirect paths correctly.

Final code:

```python
def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # If lengths are different, impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build cost matrix for characters a-z
    # cost[i][j] = minimum cost to change character i to character j
    # Use 0-25 for a-z
    cost = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal to 0
    for i in range(26):
        cost[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process operations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a = line[0]
        b = line[1]
        w = int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        if w < cost[a_idx][b_idx]:
            cost[a_idx][b_idx] = w
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if cost[i][k] + cost[k][j] < cost[i][j]:
                    cost[i][j] = cost[i][k] + cost[k][j]
    
    # Calculate total minimum cost and build result string
    total_cost = 0

        if s[i] == t[i]:
```

def solve():
    # Read input
    s = input().strip()
    t = input().strip()
    
    # If lengths are different, impossible
    if len(s) != len(t):
        print(-1)
        return
    
    n = int(input().strip())
    
    # Build cost matrix for characters a-z
    # cost[i][j] = minimum cost to change character i to character j
    # Use 0-25 for a-z
    cost = [[float('inf')] * 26 for _ in range(26)]
    
    # Initialize diagonal to 0
    for i in range(26):
        cost[i][i] = 0
    
    # Process operations
    for _ in range(n):
        line = input().strip().split()
        a = line[0]
        b = line[1]
        w = int(line[2])
        a_idx = ord(a) - ord('a')
        b_idx = ord(b) - ord('a')
        if w < cost[a_idx][b_idx]:
            cost[a_idx][b_idx] = w
    
    # Floyd-Warshall to find shortest paths between all character pairs
    for k in range(26):
        for i in range(26):
            for j in range(26):
                if cost[i][k] + cost[k][j] < cost[i][j]:
                    cost[i][j] = cost[i][k] + cost[k][j]
    
    # Calculate total minimum cost and build result string
    total_cost = 0
    result = []
    
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i] == t[i]:
            result.append(s[i])
        else:
            min_cost = float('inf')
            best_char = None
            
            # Try each possible character c
            for c_idx in range(26):
                c_char = chr(ord('a') + c_idx)
                current_cost = cost[ord(s[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx] + cost[ord(t[i]) - ord('a')][c_idx]
                if current_cost < min_cost:
                    min_cost = current_cost
                    best_char = c_char
            
            total_cost += min_cost
            result.append(best_char)
    
    # Check if total cost is finite
    if total_cost == float('inf'):
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(total_cost)
        print(''.join(result))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Petya loves lucky numbers. We all know that lucky numbers are the positive integers whose decimal representations contain only the lucky digits 4 and 7. For example, numbers 47, 744, 4 are lucky and 5, 17, 467 are not.

One day Petya encountered a tree with n vertexes. Besides, the tree was weighted, i. e. each edge of the tree has weight (a positive integer). An edge is lucky if its weight is a lucky number. Note that a tree with n vertexes is an undirected connected graph that has exactly n - 1 edges.

Petya wondered how many vertex triples (i, j, k) exists that on the way from i to j, as well as on the way from i to k there must be at least one lucky edge (all three vertexes are pairwise distinct). The order of numbers in the triple matters, that is, the triple (1, 2, 3) is not equal to the triple (2, 1, 3) and is not equal to the triple (1, 3, 2). 

Find how many such triples of vertexes exist.

Input

The first line contains the single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of tree vertexes. Next n - 1 lines contain three integers each: ui vi wi (1 ≤ ui, vi ≤ n, 1 ≤ wi ≤ 109) — the pair of vertexes connected by the edge and the edge's weight.

Output

On the single line print the single number — the answer.

Please do not use the %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit numbers in С++. It is recommended to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specificator.

Examples

Input

4
1 2 4
3 1 2
1 4 7


Output

16


Input

4
1 2 4
1 3 47
1 4 7447


Output

24

Note

The 16 triples of vertexes from the first sample are: (1, 2, 4), (1, 4, 2), (2, 1, 3), (2, 1, 4), (2, 3, 1), (2, 3, 4), (2, 4, 1), (2, 4, 3), (3, 2, 4), (3, 4, 2), (4, 1, 2), (4, 1, 3), (4, 2, 1), (4, 2, 3), (4, 3, 1), (4, 3, 2).

In the second sample all the triples should be counted: 4·3·2 = 24.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

```python
def is_lucky(weight):
    s = str(weight)
    for c in s:
        if c != '4' and c != '7':
            return False
    return True

n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list for non-lucky edges
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v, w = map(int, input().split())
        if not is_lucky(w):
            adj[u].append(v)
            adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    component_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    def dfs(v, comp):
        visited[v] = True
        size = 1
        for neighbor in adj[v]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                size += dfs(neighbor, comp)
        return size
    
    total_triples = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # Find the component containing i
            comp_size = dfs(i, 0)
            # Update all vertices in this component
            # We need to mark which vertices are in this component and get their sizes
            # Actually, we need to run DFS once per component and record sizes for all vertices in it
            pass
    
    # Let's restart: we need to find component size for each vertex
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    component_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    def dfs(v, comp_id):
        visited[v] = True
        size = 1
        for neighbor in adj[v]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                sub_size = dfs(neighbor, comp_id)
                size += sub_size
        return size
    
    # First, find all components and get size for each vertex
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    comp_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # Find the component size starting from i
            comp_size_in_component = dfs(i, 0)
            # But we need to know which vertices are in this component
            # We'll do a DFS that returns the size and marks the component
            pass
    
    # Better: do DFS that marks the component and computes size for each node
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    component_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    def dfs_count(v):
        visited[v] = True
        size = 1
        for neighbor in adj[v]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                size += dfs_count(neighbor)
        return size
    
    # Run DFS to find all components
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # Do DFS from i to get all vertices in its component
            # But we need to know component sizes for each vertex in it
            stack = [i]
            component_vertices = []
            visited_temp = [False] * (n+1)
            visited_temp[i] = True
            while stack:
                v = stack.pop()
                component_vertices.append(v)
                for neighbor in adj[v]:
                    if not visited_temp[neighbor]:
                        visited_temp[neighbor] = True
                        stack.append(neighbor)
            
            # Now, for each vertex in component_vertices, set component_size[v] = size of component
            # First find total size
            total_size = len(component_vertices)
            for v in component_vertices:
                component_size[v] = total_size
    
    # Now compute the answer
    total_triples = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if component_size[i] == 0:
            # Should not happen
            pass
        m = n - component_size[i]
        if m > 0:
            total_triples += m * (m - 1)
        # If m == 0, then n - component_size[i] = 0, so 0 * (-1) = 0, so no problem
    
    print(total_triples)
```

Wait, I see an issue. In the DFS for finding components, when I do `visited_temp[i] = True`, then I mark all vertices in the component. But when I compute `component_size[v]`, I need to count only the vertices in the component.

But if I run DFS on the non-lucky edge graph, then yes, the component size for each vertex in that component is the total size of that component.

So the above should work.

Let me test with the first example:
- Non-lucky edges: only edge (3,1) with weight 2
- So graph: 1-3, 2 isolated, 4 isolated
- Components:
  - Component with 1,3: size 2, so component_size[1]=2, component_size[3]=2
  - Component with 2: size 1, so component_size[2]=1  
  - Component with 4: size 1, so component_size[4]=1

For each i:
- i=1: m = 4-2 = 2, pairs = 2*1 = 2
- i=2: m = 4-1 = 3, pairs = 3*2 = 6
- i=3: m = 4-2 = 2, pairs = 2*1 = 2
- i=4: m = 4-1 = 3, pairs = 3*2 = 6
- Total = 2+6+2+6 = 16 ✓

Perfect.

Edge case: n=1
- Then there are no triples of distinct vertices, so answer should be 0
- In our formula, when n=1, for i=1: m = 1 - component_size[1], component_size[1] should be 1, so m=0, pairs = 0*(-1) = 0 ✓

So code is correct.

One more thing: efficiency. n ≤ 10^5, and we're doing DFS once per component, which is O(n), so it's fine.

Let me write the final code:

```python
def is_lucky(weight):
    s = str(weight)
    for c in s:
        if c != '4' and c != '7':
            return False
    return True

n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list for non-lucky edges
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v, w = map(int, input().split())
        if not is_lucky(w):
            adj[u].append(v)
            adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find connected components and component size for each vertex
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    component_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    # Use BFS or DFS to find components
    from collections import deque
    
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # BFS to find all vertices in this component
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            component_vertices = []
            component_vertices.append(i)
            
            while queue:
                v = queue.popleft()
                for neighbor in adj[v]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
                        component_vertices.append(neighbor)
            
            # All vertices in component_vertices form a component with size len(component_vertices)
            size = len(component_vertices)
            for v in component_vertices:
                component_size[v] = size
    
    # Count the triples
    total_triples = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        m = n - component_size[i]
        if m > 0:
            total_triples += m * (m - 1)
        # If m == 0 or m < 0, it contributes 0
    
    print(total_triples)
```

Wait, I used BFS but I marked visited in the outer loop, so I need to be careful.

Actually, in the BFS I'm modifying visited, and I'm using a new visited array. The loop starts from 1 to n, and I mark visited when I first encounter a vertex. So each vertex is processed exactly once.

But the BFS might not include all vertices if the graph is disconnected, but since we're iterating through all vertices from 1 to n, and using unvisited flag, it should cover all components.

Yes, this is correct.

But I can simplify: we don't need to store component_vertices, we can just compute the size in the BFS.

Actually, we can do it with a single BFS that computes the size.

But the above code is fine.

- If one edge, check if lucky

- So for each i:
  - i=1: m = 2-2 = 0 → 0
  - i=2: m = 2-2 = 0 → 0
  - total = 0

But is that correct?

For two vertices, we need triples (i,j,k) with i,j,k distinct. But there are only 2 vertices, so impossible to have 3 distinct vertices. So answer should be 0 ✓

Another case: two vertices with a lucky edge. Then we remove the edge, so each vertex is in its own component.

```python
def is_lucky(weight):
    s = str(weight)
    for c in s:
        if c != '4' and c != '7':
            return False
    return True

n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list for non-lucky edges
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v, w = map(int, input().split())
        if not is_lucky(w):
            adj[u].append(v)
            adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find connected components and component size for each vertex
    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    component_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            # BFS to find all vertices in this component
            queue = [i]
            visited[i] = True
            component_vertices = [i]
            
            while queue:
                v = queue.pop(0)
                for neighbor in adj[v]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        visited[neighbor] = True
                        queue.append(neighbor)
                        component_vertices.append(neighbor)

            size = len(component_vertices)
            for v in component_vertices:
                component_size[v] = size
    
    # Count the triples
    total_triples = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        m = n - component_size[i]
        total_triples += m * (m - 1)
    
    print(total_triples)
```

```python
def is_lucky(weight):

        if c != '4' and c != '7':

    return True

if n == 1:

    visited = [False] * (n+1)
    component_size = [0] * (n+1)
    
    for i in range(1, n+1):

            # BFS to find all vertices in this component
            from collections import deque
            queue = deque([i])
            visited[i] = True
            size = 0
            component_vertices = []
            
            while queue:

                size += 1
                component_vertices.append(v)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Petya loves lucky numbers. We all know that lucky numbers are the positive integers whose decimal representations contain only the lucky digits 4 and 7. For example, numbers 47, 744, 4 are lucky and 5, 17, 467 are not.

One day Petya encountered a tree with n vertexes. Besides, the tree was weighted, i. e. each edge of the tree has weight (a positive integer). An edge is lucky if its weight is a lucky number. Note that a tree with n vertexes is an undirected connected graph that has exactly n - 1 edges.

Petya wondered how many vertex triples (i, j, k) exists that on the way from i to j, as well as on the way from i to k there must be at least one lucky edge (all three vertexes are pairwise distinct). The order of numbers in the triple matters, that is, the triple (1, 2, 3) is not equal to the triple (2, 1, 3) and is not equal to the triple (1, 3, 2). 

Find how many such triples of vertexes exist.

Input

The first line contains the single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of tree vertexes. Next n - 1 lines contain three integers each: ui vi wi (1 ≤ ui, vi ≤ n, 1 ≤ wi ≤ 109) — the pair of vertexes connected by the edge and the edge's weight.

Output

On the single line print the single number — the answer.

Please do not use the %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit numbers in С++. It is recommended to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specificator.

Examples

Input

4
1 2 4
3 1 2
1 4 7


Output

16


Input

4
1 2 4
1 3 47
1 4 7447


Output

24

Note

The 16 triples of vertexes from the first sample are: (1, 2, 4), (1, 4, 2), (2, 1, 3), (2, 1, 4), (2, 3, 1), (2, 3, 4), (2, 4, 1), (2, 4, 3), (3, 2, 4), (3, 4, 2), (4, 1, 2), (4, 1, 3), (4, 2, 1), (4, 2, 3), (4, 3, 1), (4, 3, 2).

In the second sample all the triples should be counted: 4·3·2 = 24.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n vertices and weighted edges
2. An edge is "lucky" if its weight contains only digits 4 and 7
3. We need to count triples (i, j, k) where i, j, k are pairwise distinct vertices such that:
   - On the path from i to j, there's at least one lucky edge
   - On the path from i to k, there's at least one lucky edge
4. Order matters, so (1,2,3) is different from (2,1,3)

## Key Insights

1. In a tree, there's exactly one path between any two vertices
2. For a triple (i,j,k), we need both paths i→j and i→k to contain at least one lucky edge
3. This means vertex i must be connected to both j and k through paths that include lucky edges

## Approach

Instead of directly counting valid triples, I'll use complementary counting:

Total possible triples = n × (n-1) × (n-2)  (since all three vertices are distinct and order matters)

Invalid triples = triples where either the path i→j OR the path i→k has NO lucky edges

But we need to be careful about the condition. A triple is invalid if:
- Path i→j has no lucky edge, OR
- Path i→k has no lucky edge

So valid triples = total triples - (triples where i→j has no lucky edge) - (triples where i→k has no lucky edge) + (triples where both i→j and i→k have no lucky edges)  (by inclusion-exclusion)

Actually, let me think differently. For a fixed vertex i, when does a path from i to some vertex v have no lucky edge?

A path from i to v has no lucky edge if and only if all edges on that path have non-lucky weights.

So I can:
1. For each vertex i, find all vertices that are "reachable without lucky edges" from i (i.e., in the same connected component when we remove all lucky edges)
2. For a fixed i, let S_i be the set of vertices reachable from i without using any lucky edge
3. Then, for this i, the number of valid pairs (j,k) where both j and k are not in S_i and j ≠ k is:
   - Total pairs from vertices not in S_i: (n - |S_i|) × (n - |S_i| - 1)
4. But wait - we need both i→j and i→k to have at least one lucky edge, so j and k should NOT be in S_i (or at least not reachable without lucky edges)

Actually, if j is in S_i, then the path from i to j has no lucky edge. So for j to satisfy the condition, j must NOT be in S_i.

So for a fixed i, the number of valid (j,k) pairs where j ≠ k and both j,k ∉ S_i is:
- Total vertices not in S_i: total = n - |S_i|
- Number of ways to choose j and k from these vertices: (n - |S_i|) × (n - |S_i| - 1)

Therefore, total valid triples = Σ(i from 1 to n) [(n - |S_i|) × (n - |S_i| - 1)]

Wait, let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: n=4, edges: (1,2,4), (3,1,2), (1,4,7)
- Lucky edges: edge (1,2,4) has weight 4 (only 4) → lucky, edge (1,4,7) has weight 7 → lucky, edge (3,1,2) has weight 2 → not lucky
- So lucky edges: (1,2) and (1,4)

Let's find S_i for each i:

For i=1:
- Remove lucky edges: remove (1,2) and (1,4)
- Remaining edges: (3,1,2) - weight 2 not lucky, so still exists
- From 1, we can reach: 1 itself, 3 (via edge (3,1,2))
- So S_1 = {1,3}, |S_1| = 2
- Vertices not in S_1: {2,4}, count = 2
- Valid pairs: 2 × 1 = 2

For i=2:
- Remove lucky edges: remove (1,2) 
- Remaining: (3,1,2) and (1,4,7)
- From 2: can go to 1 (via edge with weight 4, which is lucky, so we removed it for this component), so actually when removing lucky edges, only non-lucky edges remain in the graph
- After removing lucky edges: only edge (3,1,2) remains
- From 2, we can reach: 2 itself, and 1 via non-lucky edge? No - edge (1,2) is lucky so removed, no edge from 2 to 1
- So from 2, only vertex 2 itself
- S_2 = {2}, |S_2| = 1
- Vertices not in S_2: {1,3,4}, count = 3
- Valid pairs: 3 × 2 = 6

Wait, I think I made a mistake. When we consider connectivity without lucky edges, we only consider the non-lucky edges that remain.

For i=2:
- After removing lucky edges, we remove edges with lucky weights
- Original edges: (1,2,4) - lucky → remove, (3,1,2) - not lucky → keep, (1,4,7) - lucky → remove
- So only edge (3,1,2) remains
- From vertex 2: does it have any edge left? No, the edge (1,2) is removed
- So 2 is isolated
- S_2 = {2}, |S_2| = 1
- Vertices not in S_2: {1,3,4}, count = 3
- Valid pairs: 3 × 2 = 6

For i=3:
- Remove lucky edges: remove (1,2,4) and (1,4,7)
- Remaining: (3,1,2) - not lucky → keep
- From 3: can go to 1 (via edge with weight 2)
- From 1: can go to? Edges to 2 and 4 are removed (lucky), so only to 3
- So from 3, reachable vertices: 3,1
- S_3 = {1,3}, |S_3| = 2
- Vertices not in S_3: {2,4}, count = 2
- Valid pairs: 2 × 1 = 2

For i=4:
- Remove lucky edges: remove (1,2,4) and (1,4,7)
- Remaining: (3,1,2) - not lucky → keep
- From 4: edge to 1 is lucky → removed
- So 4 is isolated
- S_4 = {4}, |S_4| = 1
- Vertices not in S_4: {1,2,3}, count = 3
- Valid pairs: 3 × 2 = 6

Total valid triples = 2 + 6 + 2 + 6 = 16 ✓

Perfect! This matches the first example.

So the algorithm:
1. Build the tree graph
2. For each vertex i, create a graph with only non-lucky edges
3. Find all vertices reachable from i in this non-lucky graph (including i itself)
4. Let |S_i| be the size of this reachable set
5. Add (n - |S_i|) * (n - |S_i| - 1) to the total

Wait, actually we need to be careful about what "reachable" means. In the non-lucky graph, we can only traverse edges that are not lucky. So from vertex i, we can reach any vertex j if there's a path from i to j using only non-lucky edges.

In a tree, this is just the connected component of i in the graph with only non-lucky edges.

For each vertex i, we need to find the size of the connected component containing i in the subgraph of non-lucky edges.

Then for each i, valid pairs (j,k) = (n - size_i) * (n - size_i - 1)

Let me verify with the second example:
n=4, edges: (1,2,4), (1,3,47), (1,4,7447)
- All weights: 4, 47, 7447 - all contain only 4 and 7 → all are lucky
- So no non-lucky edges

For each i:
- When we remove all lucky edges, the graph becomes disconnected: each vertex is isolated
- So for each i, S_i = {i}, size_i = 1
- n - size_i = 3
- Valid pairs for each i: 3 * 2 = 6
- Total = 4 * 6 = 24 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm:

1. Read input
2. For each vertex i from 1 to n:
   a. Create a graph with only non-lucky edges
   b. Find the connected component of i in this graph (using DFS/BFS or union-find)
   c. Let size = size of component containing i
   d. Add (n - size) * (n - size - 1) to result
3. Output result

But note: we need to build the graph with non-lucky edges only once, then run BFS/DFS for each vertex.

Actually, we can build the graph once, then for each vertex i, run BFS/DFS on the non-lucky subgraph starting from i to find the component size.

Since n ≤ 10^5, and each vertex might require a BFS, worst case O(n²). This might be too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

Alternative idea: Instead of doing BFS for each vertex, we can precompute the connected components of the non-lucky graph using union-find or just do one BFS/DFS for the entire non-lucky graph, then for each vertex, we just look up which component it belongs to.

Steps:
1. Build the original graph
2. Identify all non-lucky edges (weight contains only digits 4 and 7)
3. Build a graph with only non-lucky edges
4. Find all connected components in this graph
5. For each component, record the size
6. For each vertex i, find which component it belongs to, get its size, then add (n - size) * (n - size - 1) to result

This is O(n) after precomputation.

Let me implement this.

```python
def has_only_4_7(num):
    s = str(num)
    for c in s:
        if c != '4' and c != '7':
            return False
    return True

n = int(input())
edges = []
graph = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]

for _ in range(n-1):
    u, v, w = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((u, v, w))
    graph[u].append((v, w))
    graph[v].append((u, w))

# Build a graph with only non-lucky edges
non_lucky_graph = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
for u, v, w in edges:
    if not has_only_4_7(w):  # non-lucky edge
        non_lucky_graph[u].append(v)
        non_lucky_graph[v].append(u)

# Find connected components in the non-lucky graph
visited = [False] * (n+1)
components = {}  # component_id -> size
comp_id = 0

def dfs(v, comp_id):
    stack = [v]
    visited[v] = True
    component = []
    while stack:
        node = stack.pop()
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in non_lucky_graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                stack.append(neighbor)
    return component

# Find all components
for i in range(1, n+1):
    if not visited[i]:
        comp_id += 1
        comp = dfs(i, comp_id)
        for node in comp:
            components[node] = len(comp)

# For each vertex i, find its component size
result = 0
for i in range(1, n+1):
    size = components[i]
    valid_pairs = (n - size) * (n - size - 1)
    result += valid_pairs

print(result)
```

Wait, I see a bug in the component recording. I'm assigning components to each node, but I'm using a single comp_id which is not necessary for the actual size. Actually, for each node, I just need the size of its component.

But my DFS returns the component and I store the size in components[i] for each node i.

Actually, when I do dfs(i), I get a list of nodes in that component, then I can set components[node] = len(comp) for each node in the component.

But when I call dfs(i), it might not be the first unvisited, so I need to do:

```python
visited = [False] * (n+1)
components = {}  # node -> size of its component

for i in range(1, n+1):
    if not visited[i]:
        stack = [i]
        visited[i] = True
        component_nodes = []
        while stack:
            node = stack.pop()
            component_nodes.append(node)
            for neighbor in non_lucky_graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    visited[neighbor] = True
                    stack.append(neighbor)
        
        component_size = len(component_nodes)
        for node in component_nodes:
            components[node] = component_size
```

Then for each vertex i, components[i] gives the size of the component containing i.

Yes, this is correct.

Let me test with first example:
- Non-lucky edges: only edge (3,1,2) with weight 2 → non-lucky
- So non_lucky_graph:
  - 1: [3]
  - 3: [1]
  - 2: []
  - 4: []
- Components:
  - Start at 1: component [1,3], size=2
  - Then 2: not visited, component [2], size=1
  - Then 4: not visited, component [4], size=1
- So:
  - components[1]=2, components[3]=2
  - components[2]=1, components[4]=1
- For i=1: (4-2)*(4-2-1) = 2*1 = 2
- For i=2: (4-1)*(4-1-1) = 3*2 = 6
- For i=3: (4-2)*(4-2-1) = 2*1 = 2  
- For i=4: (4-1)*(4-1-1) = 3*2 = 6
- Total = 2+6+2+6 = 16 ✓

Perfect.

Let me write the final code:

```python
def has_only_4_7(num):
    s = str(num)
    for c in s:
        if c != '4' and c != '7':
            return False
    return True

n = int(input())
edges = []
graph = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]

for _ in range(n-1):
    u, v, w = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((u, v, w))
    graph[u].append((v, w))
    graph[v].append((u, w))

# Build a graph with only non-lucky edges
non_lucky_graph = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
for u, v, w in edges:
    if not has_only_4_7(w):  # non-lucky edge
        non_lucky_graph[u].append(v)
        non_lucky_graph[v].append(u)

# Find connected components in the non-lucky graph
visited = [False] * (n+1)
components = {}  # node -> size of its component

for i in range(1, n+1):
    if not visited[i]:
        stack = [i]
        visited[i] = True
        component_nodes = []
        while stack:
            node = stack.pop()
            component_nodes.append(node)
            for neighbor in non_lucky_graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    visited[neighbor] = True
                    stack.append(neighbor)
        
        component_size = len(component_nodes)
        for node in component_nodes:
            components[node] = component_size

# For each vertex i, add (n - size) * (n - size - 1) to result
result = 0
for i in range(1, n+1):
    size = components[i]
    valid_pairs = (n - size) * (n - size - 1)
    result += valid_pairs

print(result)
```

```python
def has_only_4_7(num):

    for c in s:

            return False
    return True

edges = []

    graph[u].append((v, w))
    graph[v].append((u, w))

# Build a graph with only non-lucky edges

for u, v, w in edges:
    if not has_only_4_7(w):  # non-lucky edge
        non_lucky_graph[u].append(v)
        non_lucky_graph[v].append(u)

# Find connected components in the non-lucky graph
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Petya loves lucky numbers. We all know that lucky numbers are the positive integers whose decimal representations contain only the lucky digits 4 and 7. For example, numbers 47, 744, 4 are lucky and 5, 17, 467 are not.

One day Petya encountered a tree with n vertexes. Besides, the tree was weighted, i. e. each edge of the tree has weight (a positive integer). An edge is lucky if its weight is a lucky number. Note that a tree with n vertexes is an undirected connected graph that has exactly n - 1 edges.

Petya wondered how many vertex triples (i, j, k) exists that on the way from i to j, as well as on the way from i to k there must be at least one lucky edge (all three vertexes are pairwise distinct). The order of numbers in the triple matters, that is, the triple (1, 2, 3) is not equal to the triple (2, 1, 3) and is not equal to the triple (1, 3, 2). 

Find how many such triples of vertexes exist.

Input

The first line contains the single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of tree vertexes. Next n - 1 lines contain three integers each: ui vi wi (1 ≤ ui, vi ≤ n, 1 ≤ wi ≤ 109) — the pair of vertexes connected by the edge and the edge's weight.

Output

On the single line print the single number — the answer.

Please do not use the %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit numbers in С++. It is recommended to use the cin, cout streams or the %I64d specificator.

Examples

Input

4
1 2 4
3 1 2
1 4 7


Output

16


Input

4
1 2 4
1 3 47
1 4 7447


Output

24

Note

The 16 triples of vertexes from the first sample are: (1, 2, 4), (1, 4, 2), (2, 1, 3), (2, 1, 4), (2, 3, 1), (2, 3, 4), (2, 4, 1), (2, 4, 3), (3, 2, 4), (3, 4, 2), (4, 1, 2), (4, 1, 3), (4, 2, 1), (4, 2, 3), (4, 3, 1), (4, 3, 2).

In the second sample all the triples should be counted: 4·3·2 = 24.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```
